**C# 7.0 in a Nutshell - Summary for Coding Interview Preparation**

This book serves as a comprehensive reference for C# 7.0, targeting intermediate to advanced programmers. It covers essential concepts and use cases, making it suitable for both sequential reading and random browsing. Key topics include syntax, types, variables, concurrency, security, and core .NET Framework assemblies.

**Key Features:**
- Focuses on C# language and .NET Framework, ideal for those preparing for coding interviews.
- Organized into chapters that start with basic syntax and progress to advanced topics.
- Includes practical examples and code snippets, which can be tested using LINQPad or Microsoft Visual Studio.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Familiarize yourself with C# syntax and core concepts.
- Practice coding examples provided in the book to solidify understanding.
- Explore advanced topics like concurrency and memory management, as these are often discussed in interviews.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad for testing code snippets.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for additional information on types and members.

This book is a valuable resource for anyone looking to deepen their understanding of C# and prepare effectively for coding interviews.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary**

This summary focuses on key concepts from C# 7.0 that are essential for coding interview preparation. 

**Core Concepts:**
- **C# Overview**: A type-safe, object-oriented language designed for programmer productivity, balancing simplicity and performance.
- **Object Orientation**: Key features include encapsulation, inheritance, and polymorphism. C# has a unified type system, classes, interfaces, properties, methods, and events.
- **Type Safety**: C# enforces static typing at compile time, reducing runtime errors and enhancing code reliability. The dynamic keyword allows for some dynamic typing.
- **Memory Management**: Automatic memory management via garbage collection simplifies memory handling, while pointers are available for performance-critical tasks.
- **Platform Support**: C# is now cross-platform, supporting development on Windows, Linux, macOS, iOS, and Android through frameworks like Xamarin and ASP.NET Core.
- **CLR and Managed Code**: C# compiles to Intermediate Language (IL) for execution by the Common Language Runtime (CLR), which provides features like memory management and exception handling.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Master C# syntax and core concepts, focusing on object-oriented principles.
- Practice coding examples and problems related to type safety, memory management, and platform-specific features.
- Familiarize yourself with the CLR and managed code concepts, as they are often discussed in interviews.

**Resources:**
- Utilize LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into C# features and best practices.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary**

This summary highlights key concepts from C# 7.0 and the .NET Framework that are crucial for coding interview preparation.

**Core Concepts:**
- **CLR and .NET Framework**: The Common Language Runtime (CLR) is part of the .NET Framework, which includes core libraries known as the Base Class Library (BCL). Understanding the structure and purpose of these libraries is essential.
- **Framework Variants**: Familiarize yourself with different frameworks like UWP for Windows apps, .NET Core for cross-platform applications, and Xamarin for mobile development. Each has unique features and platform support.
- **WinRT Integration**: C# can interoperate with Windows Runtime (WinRT), which enhances the development of Windows applications. Understanding how to leverage WinRT libraries can be beneficial.

**New Features in C# 7.0**:
- **Numeric Literals**: Use underscores for readability in numeric literals and binary literals with the `0b` prefix.
- **Out Variables**: Simplified syntax for declaring out parameters directly in method calls.
- **Pattern Matching**: Enhanced `is` operator for type checking and pattern variables, along with improved switch statements that support patterns.
- **Local Methods**: Ability to define methods within other methods, which can access local variables.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Master the core concepts of the CLR and .NET Framework, focusing on the BCL.
- Practice coding examples that utilize new C# 7.0 features, especially those related to out variables and pattern matching.
- Understand the differences between various frameworks and their applications.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into C# features and best practices.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on C# and its frameworks.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary**

This summary highlights essential features and concepts from C# 7.0 that are crucial for coding interview preparation.

**Key Features:**
- **Expression-bodied Members**: C# 7 extends the expression-bodied syntax to constructors, read/write properties, and finalizers, allowing for more concise code.
- **Deconstructors**: Introduces the deconstructor pattern, enabling the assignment of class fields back to variables, enhancing code readability.
- **Tuples**: Provides explicit support for tuples, allowing functions to return multiple values easily and enabling named elements for better clarity.
- **Throw Expressions**: Allows `throw` to be used as an expression, improving error handling in concise ways.
- **Micro-optimizations**: Includes features like ref locals and returns, enhancing performance in specific scenarios.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using new C# 7 features such as tuples and deconstructors in coding exercises.
- Understand the implications of expression-bodied members and throw expressions in writing clean, efficient code.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary**

This summary highlights essential features and concepts from C# 7.0 that are crucial for coding interview preparation.

**Core Concepts:**
- **Query Expressions**: Simplifies LINQ queries, especially with multiple sequences.
- **Expression Trees**: Allows lambda expressions to be represented as code DOMs, enabling remote execution of LINQ queries.
- **Automatic Properties**: Reduces boilerplate code for properties by auto-generating get/set methods.
- **Partial Methods**: Provides hooks for customization in auto-generated classes.

**Key Features from Previous Versions:**
- **C# 2.0**: Introduced generics, nullable types, iterators, and anonymous methods, which are foundational for LINQ.
- **C# 3.0**: Added LINQ support, enhancing data querying capabilities.

**C# Language Basics:**
- **Syntax**: C# syntax is similar to C/C++. Key elements include identifiers, keywords, and method declarations.
- **Compilation**: C# code is compiled into assemblies (.exe or .dll) using the C# compiler (csc.exe).

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice coding examples that utilize query expressions and expression trees.
- Understand automatic properties and partial methods for efficient coding.
- Familiarize yourself with C# syntax and compilation processes.

This summary outlines essential C# 7.0 concepts and features relevant for coding interview preparation.

**Core Concepts:**

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary**

This summary focuses on key concepts from C# 7.0 essential for coding interview preparation, particularly regarding value types, reference types, and numeric types.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Value Types vs. Reference Types:**
   - **Value Types:** Store actual data. Examples include numeric types (int, float), char, and custom structs. Assignment copies the value, leading to independent instances.
   - **Reference Types:** Store references to objects. Examples include classes and arrays. Assignment copies the reference, meaning changes to one reference affect all references to the same object.

2. **Null Handling:**
   - Reference types can be assigned `null`, indicating no object reference. Value types cannot be null unless using nullable types.

3. **Memory Management:**
   - Value types occupy memory equal to their fields. Reference types have additional overhead for storing references and object metadata.

4. **Numeric Types:**
   - C# supports various numeric types, including signed (sbyte, short, int, long) and unsigned (byte, ushort, uint, ulong) integers, as well as floating-point types (float, double, decimal). 
   - Numeric literals can be expressed in decimal, hexadecimal, and binary formats, with C# 7 allowing underscores for readability.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand the differences between value and reference types, focusing on memory management and assignment behavior.
- Practice coding examples that utilize both types, especially in scenarios involving null references and memory allocation.
- Familiarize yourself with numeric types and their usage, including how to declare and manipulate numeric literals.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into C# features and best practices.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on C# language fundamentals and memory management concepts.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary**

This summary focuses on key concepts and features of C# 7.0 relevant for coding interview preparation, particularly around numeric types and conversions.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Numeric Types and Suffixes:**
   - Numeric literals can have suffixes to explicitly define their types:
     - `F` for float (e.g., `float f = 1.0F;`)
     - `D` for double (e.g., `double d = 1D;`)
     - `M` for decimal (e.g., `decimal d = 1.0M;`)
     - `U` for uint, `L` for long, and `UL` for ulong.
   - Understanding when to use these suffixes is crucial, especially for float and decimal types.

2. **Numeric Conversions:**
   - Implicit conversions occur when the destination type can represent all values of the source type (e.g., `int` to `long`).
   - Explicit conversions are required when narrowing types (e.g., `long` to `int`).
   - Floating-point types can be implicitly converted to integral types, but the reverse requires explicit casting, which truncates any fractional part.

3. **Arithmetic Operators:**
   - Basic arithmetic operations (+, -, *, /, %) are defined for numeric types, except for 8- and 16-bit integral types.
   - Division of integral types truncates remainders, and dividing by zero results in a runtime error.

4. **Overflow Handling:**
   - Arithmetic operations can overflow silently. Use the `checked` keyword to enforce overflow checking, which throws an exception on overflow.

5. **Bitwise Operators:**
   - C# supports bitwise operations (e.g., AND, OR, XOR) for integral types.

6. **Special Float and Double Values:**
   - Floating-point types have special values like NaN (Not a Number), +∞, and -∞. Use `double.IsNaN` to check for NaN values.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice coding examples that involve numeric types, conversions, and arithmetic operations.
- Familiarize yourself with overflow handling and the use of the `checked` keyword.
- Understand the implications of special float and double values in calculations.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into numeric types and conversions.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on numeric types and their operations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary**

This summary highlights key concepts and features of C# 7.0 relevant for coding interview preparation, particularly focusing on data types, operators, and string handling.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Numeric Types:**
   - **double vs. decimal**: 
     - `double` is suitable for scientific calculations (base 2 representation) and has a precision of 15-16 significant figures. 
     - `decimal` is ideal for financial calculations (base 10 representation) with a precision of 28-29 significant figures, avoiding rounding errors common in `double`.
   - **Rounding Errors**: `float` and `double` can lead to inaccuracies in financial calculations due to their base 2 representation, while `decimal` can accurately represent base 10 numbers.

2. **Boolean Type and Operators:**
   - C# uses `bool` for logical values, which can only be true or false. 
   - Equality (`==`, `!=`) and comparison operators (`<`, `>`, etc.) work for numeric types but should be used cautiously with real numbers due to potential rounding errors.

3. **Conditional Operators:**
   - The `&&` and `||` operators perform short-circuit evaluations, while `&` and `|` do not. 
   - The ternary operator (`? :`) allows for concise conditional expressions.

4. **Strings and Characters:**
   - `char` represents a Unicode character, while `string` is an immutable sequence of characters.
   - String literals can include escape sequences and can be concatenated using the `+` operator. 
   - String interpolation (using `$`) allows for embedding expressions within strings.

5. **String Comparisons:**
   - Strings do not support `<` and `>` operators; use the `CompareTo` method for comparisons.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice coding examples that utilize numeric types, focusing on the differences between `double` and `decimal`.
- Understand Boolean operations and their implications in equality and comparison.
- Familiarize yourself with string manipulation, including concatenation and interpolation.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into C# features and best practices.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on C# language fundamentals and data handling.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Arrays and Memory Management**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Arrays:**
   - Arrays are fixed-size collections of elements of the same type, stored contiguously in memory for efficient access.
   - Declared using square brackets, e.g., `char[] vowels = new char[5];`.
   - Access elements using zero-based indexing, e.g., `vowels[0] = 'a';`.
   - Use the `Length` property to determine the number of elements, which cannot be changed after creation.
   - Arrays can be initialized in one step, e.g., `char[] vowels = {'a','e','i','o','u'};`.

2. **Value Types vs. Reference Types:**
   - Value types (e.g., `int`, `struct`) store actual data, while reference types (e.g., `class`) store references to objects.
   - Arrays are always reference types, even if they contain value types.
   - Example: `Point[] a = new Point[1000];` requires instantiation of each element if `Point` is a class.

3. **Multidimensional Arrays:**
   - **Rectangular Arrays:** Fixed-size, defined with commas, e.g., `int[,] matrix = new int[3,3];`.
   - **Jagged Arrays:** Arrays of arrays, declared with successive brackets, e.g., `int[][] matrix = new int[3][];`.

4. **Bounds Checking:**
   - C# performs bounds checking on array accesses, throwing an `IndexOutOfRangeException` for invalid indices.

5. **Memory Management:**
   - The stack stores local variables and parameters, while the heap stores objects and reference types.
   - The garbage collector manages memory on the heap, deallocating objects that are no longer referenced.

6. **Definite Assignment:**
   - C# enforces that local variables must be assigned a value before being accessed, preventing uninitialized memory access.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice array manipulations, including initialization, indexing, and iteration.
- Understand the differences between value and reference types, especially in the context of arrays.
- Familiarize yourself with the implications of memory management, including stack vs. heap allocation and garbage collection.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into arrays and memory management in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on arrays and memory management in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Variables and Parameters**

This summary focuses on key concepts related to variables and parameters in C# 7.0, which are essential for coding interview preparation.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Variable Initialization:**
   - Local variables must be initialized before use, or a compile-time error occurs.
   - Fields and array elements are automatically initialized to default values (e.g., `0` for numeric types, `null` for reference types).

2. **Default Values:**
   - Each type has a default value: 
     - Reference types: `null`
     - Numeric types: `0`
     - `char`: `'\0'`
     - `bool`: `false`
   - Use the `default` keyword to obtain default values, especially useful with generics.

3. **Parameters:**
   - Parameters define the arguments required for method calls.
   - By default, parameters are passed by value, meaning a copy of the value is created.

4. **Passing by Reference:**
   - Use the `ref` modifier to pass parameters by reference, allowing the method to modify the original variable.
   - The `out` modifier is similar but requires the parameter to be assigned before the method returns.

5. **Out Variables and Discards (C# 7):**
   - You can declare variables on the fly when calling methods with `out` parameters.
   - Use underscores (`_`) to discard parameters you don't need.

6. **Params Modifier:**
   - The `params` modifier allows a method to accept a variable number of arguments of a specified type, declared as an array.

7. **Optional Parameters:**
   - Methods can have optional parameters with default values, allowing them to be omitted in calls.
   - Mandatory parameters must precede optional ones in method declarations.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice coding examples that demonstrate variable initialization, default values, and parameter passing.
- Understand the implications of passing by reference and how to use `ref` and `out` modifiers effectively.
- Familiarize yourself with the `params` and optional parameters to handle various method signatures.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into variables and parameters in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on variables and parameters in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Key Features and Concepts**

This summary highlights essential features and concepts from C# 7.0 relevant for coding interview preparation, particularly focusing on named arguments, ref locals, and expressions.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Named Arguments:**
   - Allows specifying arguments by name rather than position, enhancing code readability.
   - Example: `Foo(x: 1, y: 2);` can be called in any order, e.g., `Foo(y: 2, x: 1);`.
   - Useful with optional parameters, e.g., `Bar(d: 3);`.

2. **Ref Locals:**
   - Introduced in C# 7, allows defining a local variable that references an array element or field.
   - Example: 
     ```csharp
     int[] numbers = { 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 };
     ref int numRef = ref numbers[2];
     numRef *= 10; // Modifies numbers[2]
     ```

3. **Ref Returns:**
   - Methods can return a reference to a variable, allowing modifications to the original variable.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     static ref string GetX() => ref X;
     ```

4. **Implicitly Typed Local Variables:**
   - Use `var` for type inference during variable declaration.
   - Example: `var x = "hello";` is equivalent to `string x = "hello";`.

5. **Expressions and Operators:**
   - Expressions denote values and can be combined using operators.
   - Operators can be unary, binary, or ternary, affecting how expressions are evaluated.
   - Operator precedence and associativity determine the order of evaluation in complex expressions.

6. **Null Operators:**
   - The null coalescing operator (`??`) and null-conditional operator (`?.`) simplify null handling in expressions.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using named arguments and understand their benefits in method calls.
- Familiarize yourself with ref locals and returns, especially in optimization scenarios.
- Work on examples that utilize implicitly typed variables and expressions with various operators.
- Understand operator precedence and associativity to solve complex expressions correctly.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into C# features and best practices.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on C# 7.0 features and concepts.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Key Features and Concepts**

This summary focuses on essential C# 7.0 features relevant for coding interview preparation, particularly around null operators, statements, and control flow.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Null Coalescing Operator (`??`)**:
   - The `??` operator returns the left operand if it is non-null; otherwise, it returns a default value. 
   - Example: 
     ```csharp
     string s1 = null;
     string s2 = s1 ?? "nothing"; // s2 evaluates to "nothing"
     ```

2. **Null-conditional Operator (`?.`)**:
   - The `?.` operator allows safe access to methods and properties. If the left operand is null, it returns null instead of throwing an exception.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     System.Text.StringBuilder sb = null;
     string s = sb?.ToString(); // s evaluates to null
     ```

3. **Declaration Statements**:
   - Used to declare variables, optionally initializing them. Multiple variables can be declared in a single statement.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     string someWord = "rosebud";
     int someNumber = 42;
     ```

4. **Local Variables**:
   - Local variables must be initialized before use, and their scope is limited to the block in which they are declared.

5. **Expression Statements**:
   - These are statements that perform an action, such as assignments or method calls.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     x = 1 + 2; // Assignment expression
     Console.WriteLine(y); // Method call expression
     ```

6. **Selection Statements**:
   - C# provides `if`, `else`, and `switch` statements for conditional execution.
   - Example of `if` statement:
     ```csharp
     if (5 < 2 * 3)
         Console.WriteLine("true");
     ```

7. **Switch Statement**:
   - The `switch` statement allows branching based on the value of a variable, providing cleaner code than multiple `if` statements.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     switch (cardNumber)
     {
         case 13:
             Console.WriteLine("King");
             break;
         // Other cases...
         default:
             Console.WriteLine(cardNumber);
             break;
     }
     ```

8. **Switch with Patterns (C# 7)**:
   - C# 7 allows switching on types, enhancing the flexibility of the `switch` statement.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     static void TellMeTheType(object x)
     {
         switch (x)
         {
             case int i:
                 Console.WriteLine("Integer");
                 break;
             case string s:
                 Console.WriteLine("String");
                 break;
             // Other cases...
         }
     }
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using null operators to handle potential null values safely.
- Familiarize yourself with declaration and expression statements, ensuring you understand variable scope and initialization.
- Work on examples that utilize selection and switch statements, especially with patterns introduced in C# 7.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into C# features and best practices.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on C# 7.0 features and concepts.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Control Flow and Statements**

This summary focuses on key control flow concepts and statements in C# 7.0 that are essential for coding interview preparation.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Switch Statement:**
   - The `switch` statement allows branching based on the type of a variable. You can use pattern matching to check types and assign values.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     switch (x)
     {
         case int i:
             Console.WriteLine("It's an int!");
             Console.WriteLine($"The square of {i} is {i * i}");
             break;
         case string s:
             Console.WriteLine("It's a string");
             Console.WriteLine($"The length of {s} is {s.Length}");
             break;
         default:
             Console.WriteLine("I don't know what x is");
             break;
     }
     ```
   - You can use the `when` keyword to add conditions to cases, and multiple cases can be stacked for similar actions.

2. **Iteration Statements:**
   - C# supports several iteration statements: `while`, `do-while`, `for`, and `foreach`.
   - **While Loop:** Executes while a condition is true.
     ```csharp
     int i = 0;
     while (i < 3)
     {
         Console.WriteLine(i);
         i++;
     }
     ```
   - **Do-While Loop:** Executes at least once before checking the condition.
     ```csharp
     do
     {
         Console.WriteLine(i);
         i++;
     } while (i < 3);
     ```
   - **For Loop:** Combines initialization, condition, and iteration in one statement.
     ```csharp
     for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++)
         Console.WriteLine(i);
     ```
   - **Foreach Loop:** Iterates over elements in a collection.
     ```csharp
     foreach (char c in "beer")
         Console.WriteLine(c);
     ```

3. **Jump Statements:**
   - **Break:** Exits a loop or switch statement.
   - **Continue:** Skips the current iteration and proceeds to the next.
   - **Return:** Exits a method and can return a value.
   - **Throw:** Raises an exception to indicate an error.

4. **Miscellaneous Statements:**
   - **Using Statement:** Automatically disposes of objects implementing `IDisposable`.
   - **Lock Statement:** Ensures that a block of code is executed by only one thread at a time.

5. **Namespaces:**
   - Organize types into hierarchical domains to avoid naming conflicts.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     namespace Outer.Middle.Inner
     {
         class Class1 {}
         class Class2 {}
     }
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using switch statements with pattern matching and conditions.
- Familiarize yourself with different loop types and their use cases.
- Understand jump statements and their implications in control flow.
- Explore the use of namespaces for organizing code effectively.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into C# control flow and statements.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on control flow and statements in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Namespaces and Types**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Using Directive:**
   - The `using` directive imports a namespace, allowing access to types without fully qualified names.
   - Example: `using Outer.Middle.Inner;` allows you to use `Class1` directly.

2. **Using Static Directive:**
   - Introduced in C# 6, it allows importing static members of a class, enabling direct access without qualification.
   - Example: `using static System.Console;` lets you call `WriteLine` directly.

3. **Namespace Rules:**
   - Names declared in outer namespaces can be used unqualified within inner namespaces.
   - You can define the same type name in different namespaces, but avoid conflicts by ensuring they are not imported simultaneously.

4. **Name Hiding:**
   - If a type name appears in both an inner and outer namespace, the inner name takes precedence. Use qualification to access the outer type.

5. **Repeated Namespaces:**
   - You can repeat namespace declarations as long as type names do not conflict, allowing for organization across multiple files.

6. **Aliasing Types and Namespaces:**
   - Use aliases to avoid type-name collisions. Example: `using PropertyInfo2 = System.Reflection.PropertyInfo;`.

7. **Extern Aliases:**
   - Resolve ambiguity when two types have the same name from different assemblies using extern aliases.

8. **Namespace Alias Qualifiers:**
   - Use the `::` token to qualify namespace names, especially when conflicts arise.

9. **Classes:**
   - Classes are reference types and can include various members such as fields, methods, properties, and constructors.
   - Fields can have modifiers like `static`, `public`, `readonly`, etc., and can be initialized with default values.

10. **Methods:**
    - Methods perform actions and can return values or use parameters to receive input. The method signature must be unique within the class.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Familiarize yourself with the structure and usage of namespaces and the implications of using directives.
- Practice creating and using classes, focusing on fields and methods, including their modifiers and initialization.
- Understand how to resolve naming conflicts using aliases and extern aliases.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into namespaces and class structures in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on namespaces and types in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Creating Types**

This summary focuses on key concepts related to creating types in C# 7.0, which are essential for coding interview preparation.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Method Modifiers:**
   - Methods can have various modifiers, including:
     - **Static**: Indicates the method belongs to the class rather than instances.
     - **Access Modifiers**: Control visibility (public, private, protected, internal).
     - **Inheritance Modifiers**: Control inheritance behavior (virtual, abstract, override, sealed).
     - **Partial**: Indicates a method is part of a partial class.
     - **Unsafe/Extern**: Used for unmanaged code.
     - **Async**: Indicates the method is asynchronous.

2. **Expression-bodied Methods:**
   - Methods can be defined using a concise syntax with a fat arrow (`=>`), allowing for single-expression methods.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     int Foo(int x) => x * 2;
     ```

3. **Method Overloading:**
   - Methods can be overloaded by having the same name but different signatures (parameter types or counts).
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     void Foo(int x) {...}
     void Foo(double x) {...}
     ```

4. **Pass-by-value vs. Pass-by-reference:**
   - Method signatures include whether parameters are passed by value or by reference. 
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     void Foo(int x) {...} // pass-by-value
     void Foo(ref int x) {...} // pass-by-reference
     ```

5. **Local Methods (C# 7):**
   - Methods can be defined within other methods, allowing access to the enclosing method's variables.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     void WriteCubes() {
         int Cube(int value) => value * value * value;
         Console.WriteLine(Cube(3));
     }
     ```

6. **Instance Constructors:**
   - Constructors initialize class or struct instances and can be overloaded.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Panda {
         string name;
         public Panda(string n) => name = n;
     }
     ```

7. **Implicit Parameterless Constructors:**
   - Automatically generated if no constructors are defined. If any constructor is defined, the parameterless constructor is not generated.

8. **Constructor and Field Initialization Order:**
   - Fields are initialized in the order they are declared before the constructor executes.

9. **Nonpublic Constructors:**
   - Constructors can be nonpublic to control instance creation, often used with static factory methods.

10. **Deconstructors (C# 7):**
    - Deconstructors allow unpacking an object into its fields using a method named `Deconstruct`.
    - Example:
      ```csharp
      public void Deconstruct(out float width, out float height) {
          width = Width;
          height = Height;
      }
      ```

11. **Object Initializers:**
    - Allow setting properties or fields directly after object creation.
    - Example:
      ```csharp
      Bunny b1 = new Bunny { Name = "Bo", LikesCarrots = true };
      ```

12. **Object Initializers vs. Optional Parameters:**
    - Object initializers provide flexibility, while optional parameters can enforce immutability but may complicate future modifications.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice defining methods with various modifiers and understand their implications.
- Familiarize yourself with expression-bodied methods and method overloading.
- Understand the differences between pass-by-value and pass-by-reference.
- Work on examples involving local methods and constructors, including deconstructors and object initializers.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into creating types in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on creating types in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Creating Types and Key Concepts**

This summary focuses on essential concepts related to creating types in C# 7.0, which are crucial for coding interview preparation.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Optional Parameters:**
   - Be cautious with optional parameters in public functions to maintain binary compatibility between assembly versions. Changes in optional parameters may lead to runtime errors if the referencing assembly is not recompiled.

2. **The `this` Reference:**
   - The `this` keyword refers to the current instance of a class, allowing disambiguation between class fields and parameters.

3. **Properties:**
   - Properties encapsulate fields and provide controlled access through `get` and `set` accessors. They can be read-only, write-only, or computed from other data.
   - Automatic properties simplify property declarations by automatically generating backing fields.
   - Expression-bodied properties allow for concise syntax in property definitions.

4. **Indexers:**
   - Indexers enable array-like access to class or struct elements, allowing for natural syntax when encapsulating collections.
   - They can be defined with multiple parameters and can be read-only if the `set` accessor is omitted.

5. **Constants vs. Static Readonly Fields:**
   - Constants are static fields with values that cannot change and are evaluated at compile time. They must be initialized with a value.
   - Static readonly fields can have different values per application and are evaluated at runtime, making them more flexible for future changes.

6. **Method Modifiers:**
   - Methods can have various modifiers (e.g., static, access modifiers, async) that define their behavior and accessibility.

7. **Automatic Properties and Initializers:**
   - Automatic properties allow for simpler property definitions, and property initializers can set default values.

8. **CLR Implementation:**
   - Properties and indexers compile to specific methods (e.g., `get_XXX`, `set_XXX`, `get_Item`, `set_Item`), optimizing performance through inlining.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice defining and using properties and indexers, focusing on their accessors and modifiers.
- Understand the implications of using optional parameters and the `this` reference in class methods.
- Familiarize yourself with the differences between constants and static readonly fields, especially in terms of assembly compatibility.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into creating types and properties in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on creating types and understanding key C# features.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Creating Types and Key Concepts**

This summary outlines essential concepts related to creating types in C# 7.0, which are crucial for coding interview preparation.

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Constants:**
   - Constants should represent values that do not change. They can be declared locally within methods or as nonlocal constants with access modifiers (public, private, etc.).
   - Example of a local constant:
     ```csharp
     static void Main() {
         const double twoPI = 2 * System.Math.PI;
     }
     ```

2. **Static Constructors:**
   - A static constructor initializes a type once and must be parameterless. It is invoked automatically before the type is used.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     class Test {
         static Test() { Console.WriteLine("Type Initialized"); }
     }
     ```

3. **Static Field Initialization:**
   - Static fields are initialized in the order they are declared, just before the static constructor is called. If a static constructor throws an exception, the type becomes unusable.

4. **Static Classes:**
   - A static class can only contain static members and cannot be instantiated or subclassed. Examples include `System.Console` and `System.Math`.

5. **Finalizers:**
   - Finalizers are methods that execute before the garbage collector reclaims memory for an object. They are defined with the class name prefixed by `~`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     class Class1 {
         ~Class1() { Console.WriteLine("Finalizing"); }
     }
     ```

6. **Partial Types and Methods:**
   - Partial types allow a class definition to be split across multiple files, enabling collaboration between auto-generated and hand-authored code.
   - Partial methods consist of a definition and an implementation, where the definition can be compiled away if not implemented.

7. **The `nameof` Operator:**
   - The `nameof` operator returns the name of a symbol as a string, providing static type checking and ensuring references are updated if the symbol is renamed.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     int count = 123;
     string name = nameof(count); // name is "count"
     ```

8. **Inheritance:**
   - A class can inherit from another class, allowing reuse of functionality. A derived class (subclass) inherits members from a base class (superclass).
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Asset { public string Name; }
     public class Stock : Asset { public long SharesOwned; }
     ```

9. **Polymorphism:**
   - Polymorphism allows a variable of a base class type to refer to an object of a derived class. This enables methods to operate on base class references while utilizing derived class functionality.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public static void Display(Asset asset) { Console.WriteLine(asset.Name); }
     ```

10. **Casting and Reference Conversions:**
    - Upcasting creates a base class reference from a subclass reference, while downcasting converts a base class reference back to a subclass reference. Upcasting is safe, but downcasting requires type checking.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand the implications of constants, static constructors, and field initialization order.
- Familiarize yourself with static classes and their limitations.
- Practice writing finalizers and using partial types and methods.
- Utilize the `nameof` operator for safer code.
- Explore inheritance and polymorphism, focusing on how they enable code reuse and flexibility.
- Work on examples involving casting and reference conversions to solidify understanding.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into creating types and key C# features.

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on creating types and understanding key C# concepts.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Downcasting, Upcasting, and Inheritance**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Downcasting:**
   - Downcasting creates a subclass reference from a base class reference. It requires an explicit cast and can throw an `InvalidCastException` if the cast fails.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Stock msft = new Stock();
     Asset a = msft; // Upcast
     Stock s = (Stock)a; // Downcast
     ```

2. **The `as` Operator:**
   - The `as` operator attempts a downcast and returns `null` if it fails, avoiding exceptions. This is useful for null checks before accessing members.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Asset a = new Asset();
     Stock s = a as Stock; // s is null if a is not a Stock
     ```

3. **The `is` Operator:**
   - The `is` operator checks if an object can be cast to a specified type, often used before downcasting to prevent exceptions.
   - C# 7 introduced pattern variables, allowing you to declare a variable during the type check.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     if (a is Stock s)
         Console.WriteLine(s.SharesOwned);
     ```

4. **Virtual Function Members:**
   - Methods, properties, indexers, and events can be marked as `virtual` to allow subclasses to override them.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public virtual decimal Liability => 0; // In base class
     ```

5. **Abstract Classes and Members:**
   - Abstract classes cannot be instantiated and must be implemented by derived classes. Abstract members must be overridden in subclasses.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public abstract decimal NetValue { get; }
     ```

6. **Hiding Inherited Members:**
   - A subclass can hide a member from the base class using the `new` modifier, which suppresses compiler warnings about ambiguity.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public new int Counter = 2; // Hides base class member
     ```

7. **Sealing Functions and Classes:**
   - The `sealed` keyword prevents further overriding of a method or class. This is useful for finalizing behavior in derived classes.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public sealed override decimal Liability { get { return Mortgage; } }
     ```

8. **The `base` Keyword:**
   - The `base` keyword accesses members of the base class, allowing derived classes to call overridden methods or properties.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public override decimal Liability => base.Liability + Mortgage;
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice downcasting and upcasting, ensuring you understand the implications and error handling.
- Familiarize yourself with the `as` and `is` operators for safe type checking and casting.
- Understand the use of virtual and abstract members in inheritance, and how to hide members intentionally.
- Explore sealing methods and classes to control inheritance behavior.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into inheritance and type casting in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on downcasting, upcasting, and inheritance in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Inheritance, Constructors, and Type Handling**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Constructors and Inheritance:**
   - Subclasses must define their own constructors; they do not inherit constructors from the base class.
   - Subclasses can call base class constructors using the `base` keyword.
   - If a subclass constructor omits the `base` keyword, the parameterless constructor of the base class is called implicitly.

2. **Constructor and Field Initialization Order:**
   - Initialization occurs in the following order:
     1. Fields in the subclass are initialized.
     2. Arguments for the base class constructor are evaluated.
     3. Base class constructor body executes.
     4. Subclass constructor body executes.

3. **Method Overloading and Resolution:**
   - Method overloads are resolved at compile time based on the most specific type.
   - Casting to `dynamic` defers overload resolution to runtime, allowing the actual type to determine which overload to call.

4. **The `object` Type:**
   - `object` is the ultimate base class for all types in C#. Any type can be upcast to `object`.
   - A general-purpose stack can be implemented using `object` to hold any type, demonstrating type unification.

5. **Boxing and Unboxing:**
   - Boxing converts a value type to a reference type, while unboxing converts it back.
   - Unboxing requires an explicit cast and can throw an `InvalidCastException` if the types do not match.

6. **Static and Runtime Type Checking:**
   - C# performs static type checking at compile time and runtime type checking during downcasting or unboxing.
   - The `GetType` method retrieves the runtime type of an object, while the `typeof` operator provides the type at compile time.

7. **The `ToString` Method:**
   - The `ToString` method returns a string representation of an object. It can be overridden in custom types to provide meaningful output.

8. **Structs vs. Classes:**
   - Structs are value types and do not support inheritance (other than deriving from `System.ValueType`), while classes are reference types.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand the implications of constructors in inheritance and practice defining constructors in subclasses.
- Familiarize yourself with the initialization order of fields and constructors.
- Practice method overloading and resolution, especially with dynamic types.
- Explore boxing and unboxing, ensuring you understand the differences between value and reference types.
- Use `GetType` and `typeof` to check types and practice overriding the `ToString` method in custom classes.
- Distinguish between structs and classes, focusing on their characteristics and use cases.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into inheritance, constructors, and type handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on inheritance, constructors, and type handling in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Structs, Access Modifiers, and Interfaces**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Structs:**
   - Structs can have members like classes but cannot have:
     - A parameterless constructor
     - Field initializers
     - A finalizer
     - Virtual or protected members
   - Structs are value types, making them efficient for scenarios where value semantics are desired, such as numeric types.
   - Structs have a default constructor that zeroes fields, and custom constructors must explicitly assign all fields.

2. **Access Modifiers:**
   - Access modifiers control the visibility of types and members:
     - **public**: Accessible everywhere.
     - **internal**: Accessible only within the same assembly (default for non-nested types).
     - **private**: Accessible only within the containing type (default for class/struct members).
     - **protected**: Accessible within the containing type and derived types.
     - **protected internal**: Accessible within the same assembly or derived types.
   - Accessibility can be capped; for example, a public member in an internal class is effectively internal.

3. **Friend Assemblies:**
   - Use the `InternalsVisibleTo` attribute to expose internal members to specified friend assemblies.

4. **Interfaces:**
   - Interfaces define a contract for classes/structs without providing implementation. Members are implicitly abstract and public.
   - A class or struct can implement multiple interfaces, unlike class inheritance which is limited to one base class.
   - Example of an interface:
     ```csharp
     public interface IEnumerator {
         bool MoveNext();
         object Current { get; }
         void Reset();
     }
     ```

5. **Explicit Interface Implementation:**
   - To resolve member signature collisions, interfaces can be explicitly implemented, requiring casting to access the member.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     interface I1 { void Foo(); }
     interface I2 { int Foo(); }
     public class Widget : I1, I2 {
         public void Foo() { /* Implementation for I1 */ }
         int I2.Foo() { /* Implementation for I2 */ return 42; }
     }
     ```

6. **Inheritance and Virtual Members:**
   - Interface members are sealed by default but can be marked as virtual or abstract in implementing classes to allow overriding.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public interface IUndoable { void Undo(); }
     public class TextBox : IUndoable {
         public virtual void Undo() { /* Implementation */ }
     }
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand the limitations and use cases for structs, especially in terms of memory efficiency and value semantics.
- Familiarize yourself with access modifiers and their implications for encapsulation and accessibility.
- Practice defining and implementing interfaces, including handling explicit implementations and resolving member collisions.
- Explore inheritance in the context of interfaces and how to use virtual members effectively.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into structs, access modifiers, and interfaces in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on structs, access modifiers, and interfaces in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Interfaces, Enums, and Type Safety**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Reimplementing Interfaces in Subclasses:**
   - A subclass can reimplement an interface member from a base class, effectively overriding it. This works for both explicit and implicit implementations.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public interface IUndoable { void Undo(); }
     public class TextBox : IUndoable {
         void IUndoable.Undo() => Console.WriteLine("TextBox.Undo");
     }
     public class RichTextBox : TextBox, IUndoable {
         public void Undo() => Console.WriteLine("RichTextBox.Undo");
     }
     ```
   - Calling the `Undo` method through the interface or the subclass will invoke the subclass's implementation.

2. **Alternatives to Interface Reimplementation:**
   - Reimplementation can lead to issues, such as the inability to call the base class method. To avoid this, consider marking base class methods as `virtual` or using a protected method pattern.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class TextBox : IUndoable {
         void IUndoable.Undo() => Undo();
         protected virtual void Undo() => Console.WriteLine("TextBox.Undo");
     }
     ```

3. **Interfaces and Boxing:**
   - Boxing occurs when a struct is converted to an interface. Implicitly implemented members do not cause boxing, while explicitly implemented members do.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     struct S : I { public void Foo() {} }
     S s = new S();
     I i = s; // Boxing occurs here
     ```

4. **Writing Classes vs. Interfaces:**
   - Use classes for types that share implementations and interfaces for types with independent implementations. This helps avoid issues with multiple inheritance.
   - Example structure:
     ```csharp
     abstract class Animal {}
     class Bird : Animal {}
     interface IFlyingCreature {}
     ```

5. **Enums:**
   - Enums are special value types that define a group of named constants. They can have an underlying integral type and can be explicitly assigned values.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public enum BorderSide { Left, Right, Top, Bottom }
     ```

6. **Enum Conversions:**
   - Enums can be converted to and from their underlying integral values with explicit casting.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     int i = (int)BorderSide.Left;
     BorderSide side = (BorderSide)i;
     ```

7. **Flags Enums:**
   - Flags enums allow combining enum members using bitwise operators. Members should be assigned values in powers of two.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [Flags]
     public enum BorderSides { None = 0, Left = 1, Right = 2, Top = 4, Bottom = 8 }
     ```

8. **Type-Safety Issues:**
   - Enums can be cast to invalid values, leading to potential runtime errors. Care should be taken to ensure values remain within defined enum members.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     BorderSide b = (BorderSide)12345; // Invalid value
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice reimplementing interface members in subclasses and understand the implications of explicit vs. implicit implementations.
- Familiarize yourself with boxing and unboxing, especially in the context of structs and interfaces.
- Understand when to use classes versus interfaces based on implementation sharing.
- Work with enums, including defining, converting, and using flags enums, while being mindful of type safety.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into interfaces, enums, and type safety in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on interfaces, enums, and type safety in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Enums, Nested Types, and Generics**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Enum Validity Checks:**
   - Use `Enum.IsDefined` to check if an enum value is valid, but it does not work for flagged enums. A helper method can be created to validate flagged enums using `Enum.ToString()`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     static bool IsFlagDefined(Enum e) {
         decimal d;
         return !decimal.TryParse(e.ToString(), out d);
     }
     ```

2. **Nested Types:**
   - Nested types are declared within another type and can access the enclosing type’s private members. They can be declared with various access modifiers, with the default being private.
   - Accessing a nested type requires qualification with the enclosing type's name.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class TopLevel {
         public class Nested { }
     }
     ```

3. **Accessing Nested Types:**
   - Nested types can be accessed from outside their enclosing type using the enclosing type's name.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     TopLevel.Nested n = new TopLevel.Nested();
     ```

4. **Generics:**
   - Generics allow for code reusability across different types by using type parameters. They increase type safety and reduce the need for casting and boxing.
   - Example of a generic type:
     ```csharp
     public class Stack<T> {
         T[] data = new T[100];
         public void Push(T obj) => data[position++] = obj;
         public T Pop() => data[--position];
     }
     ```

5. **Generic Methods:**
   - Generic methods can declare type parameters within their signature, allowing for general-purpose algorithms.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     static void Swap<T>(ref T a, ref T b) {
         T temp = a;
         a = b;
         b = temp;
     }
     ```

6. **Declaring Type Parameters:**
   - Type parameters can be introduced in classes, structs, interfaces, and methods. Properties cannot introduce type parameters but can use them.
   - Example of a generic class:
     ```csharp
     class Dictionary<TKey, TValue> { }
     ```

7. **Open and Unbound Generic Types:**
   - Open generic types do not exist at runtime, but unbound generic types can be represented as `Type` objects using the `typeof` operator.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type a1 = typeof(A<>);
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice validating enum values and understand the limitations of `Enum.IsDefined`.
- Familiarize yourself with nested types and their access rules.
- Work on creating and using generic types and methods, focusing on type parameters and their benefits.
- Explore the differences between open and unbound generic types, especially in the context of reflection.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into enums, nested types, and generics in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on enums, nested types, and generics in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Generics and Type Handling**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Closed and Open Types:**
   - Use the `typeof` operator to specify closed types (e.g., `typeof(A<int, int>)`) or open types (e.g., `class B<T> { void X() { Type t = typeof(T); } }`).

2. **Default Values:**
   - The `default` keyword retrieves the default value for a generic type parameter. For reference types, it returns `null`, and for value types, it zeroes the fields.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     static void Zap<T>(T[] array) {
         for (int i = 0; i < array.Length; i++)
             array[i] = default(T);
     }
     ```

3. **Generic Constraints:**
   - Constraints can specify that a type parameter must inherit from a specific class, implement an interface, be a reference type, a value type, or have a parameterless constructor.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     class GenericClass<T, U> where T : SomeClass, Interface1 where U : new() { ... }
     ```

4. **Using IComparable for Generic Methods:**
   - Implementing the `IComparable<T>` interface allows creating generic methods like `Max` that can compare values.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     static T Max<T>(T a, T b) where T : IComparable<T> {
         return a.CompareTo(b) > 0 ? a : b;
     }
     ```

5. **Subclassing Generic Types:**
   - Generic classes can be subclassed, either leaving type parameters open or closing them with specific types.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     class SpecialStack<T> : Stack<T> { ... }
     class IntStack : Stack<int> { ... }
     ```

6. **Self-Referencing Generics:**
   - A type can reference itself as a type parameter, allowing for more specific implementations.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Balloon : IEquatable<Balloon> { ... }
     ```

7. **Static Data in Generics:**
   - Static data is unique for each closed type, meaning each instantiation of a generic type has its own static members.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     class Bob<T> { public static int Count; }
     ```

8. **Type Parameters and Conversions:**
   - C# supports various conversions, including numeric, reference, boxing/unboxing, and custom conversions. Ambiguities can arise with generics.
   - Use the `as` operator for safe casting to avoid compile-time errors.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     StringBuilder Foo<T>(T arg) {
         StringBuilder sb = arg as StringBuilder;
         if (sb != null) return sb;
     }
     ```

9. **Covariance and Contravariance:**
   - Covariance allows a type parameter to be converted to a more derived type, while contravariance allows it to be converted to a less derived type.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     IFoo<string> s = ...;
     IFoo<object> b = s; // Covariance
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using the `default` keyword and understand its implications for generic types.
- Familiarize yourself with applying constraints to generic type parameters and their benefits.
- Work on implementing generic methods using interfaces like `IComparable<T>`.
- Explore subclassing generic types and self-referencing generics for advanced type handling.
- Understand static data behavior in generics and how it differs across closed types.
- Practice type conversions and the use of covariance and contravariance in generic interfaces.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into generics and type handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on generics and type handling in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Arrays, Covariance, and Delegates**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Arrays and Covariance**:
   - Array types in C# support covariance, allowing a derived type array (e.g., `Bear[]`) to be assigned to a base type array (e.g., `Animal[]`).
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Bear[] bears = new Bear[3];
     Animal[] animals = bears; // OK
     animals[0] = new Camel(); // Runtime error
     ```

2. **Covariant Type Parameters**:
   - Type parameters in interfaces can be declared covariant using the `out` modifier, ensuring type safety.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public interface IPoppable<out T> { T Pop(); }
     var bears = new Stack<Bear>();
     IPoppable<Animal> animals = bears; // Legal
     ```

3. **Contravariance**:
   - Contravariance allows a type parameter to be converted in the reverse direction using the `in` modifier, applicable in input positions.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public interface IPushable<in T> { void Push(T obj); }
     IPushable<Animal> animals = new Stack<Animal>();
     IPushable<Bear> bears = animals; // Legal
     ```

4. **Generics**:
   - C# generics allow for type-safe code reusability. They differ from C++ templates as C# generics are compiled into libraries and resolved at runtime.
   - Example of a generic method:
     ```csharp
     static T Max<T>(T a, T b) where T : IComparable<T> => a.CompareTo(b) > 0 ? a : b;
     ```

5. **Delegates**:
   - Delegates are objects that encapsulate method references, allowing methods to be passed as parameters.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     delegate int Transformer(int x);
     Transformer t = Square;
     int result = t(3); // Invokes Square method
     ```

6. **Higher-Order Functions**:
   - Functions that take other functions as parameters or return them are called higher-order functions. The `Transform` method is an example of this.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public static void Transform(int[] values, Transformer t) { ... }
     ```

7. **Multicast Delegates**:
   - Delegates can reference multiple methods, allowing them to be invoked in sequence.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     SomeDelegate d = SomeMethod1;
     d += SomeMethod2; // Invokes both methods
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using arrays and understand covariance and its implications.
- Familiarize yourself with covariant and contravariant type parameters in interfaces.
- Work on examples of generics, focusing on type constraints and their applications.
- Understand delegates, their usage, and how to implement higher-order functions.
- Explore multicast delegates and their behavior in invoking multiple methods.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into arrays, covariance, and delegates in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on arrays, covariance, and delegates in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Delegates, Multicast Delegates, and Generics**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Delegates and Null Values:**
   - Assigning a delegate variable to `null` and then using `+=` to add a method is equivalent to directly assigning the method to the delegate.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     SomeDelegate d = null;
     d += SomeMethod1; // Equivalent to d = SomeMethod1;
     ```

2. **Immutable Nature of Delegates:**
   - Delegates are immutable; using `+=` or `-=` creates a new delegate instance rather than modifying the existing one.

3. **Multicast Delegates:**
   - A multicast delegate can reference multiple methods. When invoked, it returns the value from the last method called if the return type is non-void; previous return values are discarded.
   - Example of a multicast delegate:
     ```csharp
     public delegate void ProgressReporter(int percentComplete);
     ```

4. **Instance vs. Static Method Targets:**
   - Delegates referencing instance methods maintain a reference to the instance, accessible via the `Target` property of the delegate.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     ProgressReporter p = x.InstanceProgress;
     ```

5. **Generic Delegate Types:**
   - Delegates can have generic type parameters, allowing for flexible and reusable code.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public delegate T Transformer<T>(T arg);
     ```

6. **Func and Action Delegates:**
   - `Func` and `Action` are built-in generic delegate types that can handle methods with various return types and argument counts.
   - Example of using `Func`:
     ```csharp
     public static void Transform<T>(T[] values, Func<T, T> transformer) { ... }
     ```

7. **Delegates vs. Interfaces:**
   - Delegates are preferable when a single method is defined, multicast capability is needed, or when multiple implementations are required without creating separate types for each.
   - Example of using an interface:
     ```csharp
     public interface ITransformer { int Transform(int x); }
     ```

8. **Delegate Compatibility:**
   - Delegate types are incompatible with one another, even with the same signatures. However, delegates can be equal if they reference the same method targets.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     D1 d1 = Method1;
     D2 d2 = new D2(d1); // Allowed
     ```

9. **Parameter and Return Type Compatibility:**
   - Delegates support contravariance (more specific parameter types) and covariance (more specific return types).
   - Example of contravariance:
     ```csharp
     StringAction sa = new StringAction(ActOnObject);
     ```

10. **Generic Delegate Type Parameter Variance:**
    - When defining generic delegates, use `out` for covariant return types and `in` for contravariant parameter types to respect type inheritance.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice creating and using delegates, focusing on multicast capabilities and the implications of immutability.
- Familiarize yourself with generic delegates and their usage in various scenarios.
- Understand the differences between delegates and interfaces, and when to use each.
- Explore delegate compatibility, including parameter and return type variance.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into delegates, multicast delegates, and generics in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on delegates, multicast delegates, and generics in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Delegates and Events**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Covariant and Contravariant Delegates:**
   - Covariant delegates allow a derived type to be assigned to a base type. Example:
     ```csharp
     delegate TResult Func<out TResult>();
     Func<string> x = ...;
     Func<object> y = x; // Valid
     ```
   - Contravariant delegates allow a base type to be assigned to a derived type. Example:
     ```csharp
     delegate void Action<in T>(T arg);
     Action<object> x = ...;
     Action<string> y = x; // Valid
     ```

2. **Events:**
   - Events formalize the broadcaster/subscriber model, where a broadcaster invokes a delegate and subscribers register their methods using `+=` and `-=`. 
   - Events prevent subscribers from interfering with each other, ensuring encapsulation.

3. **Event Declaration:**
   - Declare an event using the `event` keyword:
     ```csharp
     public delegate void PriceChangedHandler(decimal oldPrice, decimal newPrice);
     public event PriceChangedHandler PriceChanged;
     ```

4. **Event Implementation:**
   - The compiler translates event declarations into private delegate fields with add/remove accessors, allowing controlled access to the delegate.
   - Example of firing an event:
     ```csharp
     if (PriceChanged != null)
         PriceChanged(oldPrice, price);
     ```

5. **Standard Event Pattern:**
   - Use `EventArgs` to convey event information. Create a subclass for specific event data:
     ```csharp
     public class PriceChangedEventArgs : EventArgs {
         public readonly decimal LastPrice;
         public readonly decimal NewPrice;
         public PriceChangedEventArgs(decimal lastPrice, decimal newPrice) {
             LastPrice = lastPrice;
             NewPrice = newPrice;
         }
     }
     ```

6. **EventHandler Delegate:**
   - Use the generic `EventHandler<TEventArgs>` delegate for events:
     ```csharp
     public event EventHandler<PriceChangedEventArgs> PriceChanged;
     ```

7. **Invoking Events:**
   - Create a protected virtual method to invoke the event, ensuring thread safety:
     ```csharp
     protected virtual void OnPriceChanged(PriceChangedEventArgs e) {
         PriceChanged?.Invoke(this, e);
     }
     ```

8. **Using Events:**
   - Example of subscribing to an event:
     ```csharp
     stock.PriceChanged += stock_PriceChanged;
     ```

9. **Nongeneric EventHandler:**
   - Use the predefined `EventHandler` delegate when no additional event data is needed:
     ```csharp
     public event EventHandler PriceChanged;
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand the differences between covariant and contravariant delegates and their applications.
- Familiarize yourself with the event model in C#, including how to declare, implement, and invoke events.
- Practice using the standard event pattern and the `EventHandler` delegate for consistency in event handling.
- Explore thread safety when invoking events, especially in multithreaded scenarios.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into delegates and events in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on delegates and events in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Events, Lambda Expressions, and Capturing Variables**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Event Accessors:**
   - Events can have explicit accessors for `+=` and `-=` operations, allowing for custom implementations. By default, the compiler generates these accessors.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     private EventHandler priceChanged;
     public event EventHandler PriceChanged {
         add { priceChanged += value; }
         remove { priceChanged -= value; }
     }
     ```

2. **Event Modifiers:**
   - Events can be marked as virtual, overridden, abstract, sealed, or static, similar to methods.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public static event EventHandler<EventArgs> StaticEvent;
     public virtual event EventHandler<EventArgs> VirtualEvent;
     ```

3. **Lambda Expressions:**
   - Lambda expressions are unnamed methods that can be used in place of delegate instances. They can be converted to delegate instances or expression trees.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Func<int, int> sqr = x => x * x;
     ```

4. **Explicitly Specifying Lambda Parameter Types:**
   - When the compiler cannot infer the type of lambda parameters, you must specify them explicitly.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Bar((int x) => Foo(x));
     ```

5. **Capturing Outer Variables:**
   - Lambda expressions can reference local variables from their enclosing method, extending their lifetime to that of the delegate.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     int factor = 2;
     Func<int, int> multiplier = n => n * factor;
     ```

6. **Captured Variables and Closures:**
   - Captured variables are evaluated when the delegate is invoked, not when captured. This can lead to unexpected results if not managed properly.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     int seed = 0;
     Func<int> natural = () => seed++;
     ```

7. **Capturing Iteration Variables:**
   - When capturing loop iteration variables, the same variable is used across iterations, which can lead to unexpected results. Use a local variable to avoid this.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
         int loopScopedI = i;
         actions[i] = () => Console.Write(loopScopedI);
     }
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand how to implement event accessors and the implications of using explicit accessors.
- Familiarize yourself with lambda expressions, their syntax, and how to specify parameter types explicitly.
- Practice capturing outer variables and managing closures to avoid common pitfalls, especially with iteration variables.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into events, lambda expressions, and variable capturing in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on events, lambda expressions, and variable capturing in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Lambda Expressions, Local Methods, and Exception Handling**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Lambda Expressions vs. Local Methods:**
   - Local methods can be recursive, avoid delegate type specification, and incur less overhead than lambda expressions.
   - Local methods access local variables directly, while lambda expressions may require variable hoisting.

2. **Anonymous Methods:**
   - Introduced in C# 2.0, anonymous methods are similar to lambda expressions but lack features like implicit typing and expression syntax.
   - They can capture outer variables and can be used for event handlers without needing a null check.

3. **Try Statements and Exception Handling:**
   - A `try` block is followed by `catch` and/or `finally` blocks to handle exceptions and perform cleanup.
   - The `catch` block can handle specific exceptions or use a general `System.Exception` to catch all errors.

4. **Catch Clause:**
   - You can have multiple `catch` clauses for different exception types, with specific handlers preceding general ones.
   - Exception filters (introduced in C# 6) allow conditional handling of exceptions.

5. **Finally Block:**
   - The `finally` block executes regardless of whether an exception occurs, ensuring cleanup tasks are performed.

6. **Using Statement:**
   - The `using` statement simplifies resource management by automatically calling `Dispose` on objects implementing `IDisposable`, ensuring proper cleanup.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice writing and using lambda expressions and local methods, focusing on their differences and use cases.
- Familiarize yourself with exception handling patterns, including the use of `try`, `catch`, and `finally` blocks.
- Understand the implications of using anonymous methods and how they compare to lambda expressions.
- Explore the `using` statement for managing resources effectively.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into lambda expressions, exception handling, and resource management in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on lambda expressions, local methods, and exception handling in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Exception Handling and Enumerations**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Throwing Exceptions:**
   - Exceptions can be thrown by the runtime or user code. For example, `Display` throws a `System.ArgumentNullException` if the input is null.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     static void Display(string name) {
         if (name == null) throw new ArgumentNullException(nameof(name));
         Console.WriteLine(name);
     }
     ```

2. **Throw Expressions (C# 7):**
   - In C# 7, `throw` can be used as an expression in expression-bodied functions and ternary conditional expressions.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public string Foo() => throw new NotImplementedException();
     string ProperCase(string value) =>
         value == null ? throw new ArgumentException("value") : 
         value == "" ? "" : 
         char.ToUpper(value[0]) + value.Substring(1);
     ```

3. **Rethrowing Exceptions:**
   - You can rethrow an exception using `throw;` to maintain the original stack trace.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     catch (Exception ex) {
         // Log error
         throw; // Rethrow same exception
     }
     ```

4. **Key Properties of System.Exception:**
   - **StackTrace:** Shows the call stack at the time the exception was thrown.
   - **Message:** Describes the error.
   - **InnerException:** Contains the original exception that caused the current exception.

5. **Common Exception Types:**
   - **System.ArgumentException:** Thrown for invalid arguments.
   - **System.ArgumentNullException:** Thrown when an argument is null.
   - **System.InvalidOperationException:** Thrown when an object's state is invalid for the operation.
   - **System.NotImplementedException:** Indicates a method is not implemented.
   - **System.ObjectDisposedException:** Thrown when a method is called on a disposed object.

6. **TryXXX Method Pattern:**
   - This pattern allows methods to return a success/failure code or throw an exception. For example, `int.TryParse` returns a boolean indicating success.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public return-type XXX(input-type input) {
         return-type returnValue;
         if (!TryXXX(input, out returnValue))
             throw new YYYException(...);
         return returnValue;
     }
     ```

7. **Enumeration and Iterators:**
   - Enumerators provide a way to iterate over a sequence of values. They implement `IEnumerator` or `IEnumerator<T>`.
   - The `foreach` statement simplifies iteration over enumerable objects.
   - Example of using `foreach`:
     ```csharp
     foreach (char c in "beer") Console.WriteLine(c);
     ```

8. **Collection Initializers:**
   - You can instantiate and populate collections in a single step using collection initializers.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     List<int> list = new List<int> { 1, 2, 3 };
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand how to throw and rethrow exceptions, including the use of throw expressions.
- Familiarize yourself with the properties of exceptions and common exception types.
- Practice implementing the TryXXX method pattern for error handling.
- Explore enumerators and the use of collection initializers for efficient code.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into exception handling and enumerations in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on exception handling and enumerations in C#.
