**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Asynchronous Programming and Continuations**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Asynchronous Programming with Tasks:**
   - Tasks are ideal for asynchronous programming, allowing for continuations that facilitate non-blocking operations.
   - Use `TaskCompletionSource` for implementing I/O-bound asynchronous methods, while `Task.Run` is suitable for compute-bound methods.

2. **Coarse-Grained vs. Fine-Grained Concurrency:**
   - Coarse-grained concurrency can be achieved by running methods like `DisplayPrimeCounts` in a separate task.
   - Fine-grained concurrency involves creating asynchronous versions of methods, such as `GetPrimesCountAsync`, to allow for better control over execution flow.

3. **Using async and await:**
   - The `async` and `await` keywords simplify asynchronous programming by allowing methods to be written in a synchronous style while still being non-blocking.
   - The compiler transforms `await` expressions into continuations, managing the execution flow and handling completion.

4. **Sequential Execution of Tasks:**
   - To ensure tasks execute sequentially, use recursive calls in continuations instead of traditional loops, which can lead to parallel execution.
   - Implementing a state machine can help manage task execution and completion.

5. **Handling Task Results and Exceptions:**
   - Use `await` to retrieve results from tasks without blocking the main thread. Handle exceptions within asynchronous methods to prevent unobserved exceptions from terminating the application.

6. **Functional Programming with LINQ:**
   - Consider using LINQ queries as an alternative to imperative loops for executing asynchronous operations, which can simplify the code structure and improve readability.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice writing asynchronous methods using `async` and `await`, and understand how to manage continuations effectively.
- Familiarize yourself with the differences between coarse-grained and fine-grained concurrency, and when to use each approach.
- Learn how to handle task results and exceptions in asynchronous programming to ensure robust applications.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into asynchronous programming and task management in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on asynchronous programming and continuations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Asynchronous Programming and Continuations**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Await Expressions:**
   - When an `await` expression is encountered, execution returns to the caller, attaching a continuation to the awaited task. Upon task completion, execution resumes in the method.
   - The awaited expression typically returns a `Task<T>`, and the result can be accessed via `GetResult()`.

2. **Capturing Local State:**
   - `await` can be used in various contexts, including loops, preserving local variables and state across asynchronous calls.
   - The compiler transforms asynchronous methods into state machines, allowing for seamless execution flow.

3. **Synchronization Context:**
   - The synchronization context ensures that execution resumes on the same thread (e.g., UI thread) after an `await`, maintaining thread affinity for UI updates.

4. **Asynchronous UI Example:**
   - In a UI application, using asynchronous methods prevents the UI from becoming unresponsive. For example, using `GetPrimesCountAsync` allows for non-blocking execution while updating UI elements.

5. **Task-Based Asynchronous Pattern:**
   - The `Task` class simplifies asynchronous programming, allowing for easy management of asynchronous operations. Use `Task.Run` for compute-bound tasks and `async/await` for I/O-bound tasks.

6. **Error Handling:**
   - Exceptions in asynchronous methods can be handled using try/catch blocks. The `finally` block executes after the method completes, ensuring cleanup.

7. **Comparison to Coarse-Grained Concurrency:**
   - Prior to C# 5, asynchronous programming relied on cumbersome patterns. The introduction of `async` and `await` simplifies the process, allowing for more intuitive asynchronous code.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice writing asynchronous methods using `async` and `await`, and understand how continuations work.
- Familiarize yourself with the synchronization context and its role in UI applications.
- Learn how to handle exceptions in asynchronous methods effectively.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into asynchronous programming in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on asynchronous programming and continuations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Asynchronous Programming and Task Management**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Using Task.Run for Asynchronous Execution:**
   - Utilize `Task.Run` to execute methods on a worker thread, allowing the UI to remain responsive. For example:
     ```csharp
     _button.Click += (sender, args) => {
         _button.IsEnabled = false;
         Task.Run(() => Go());
     };
     ```

2. **Dispatcher for UI Updates:**
   - When updating UI elements from a worker thread, use `Dispatcher.BeginInvoke` to marshal updates back to the UI thread:
     ```csharp
     Dispatcher.BeginInvoke(new Action(() => _results.Text += result + " primes..."));
     ```

3. **Race Conditions:**
   - Be cautious of race conditions when using multithreading, especially when accessing shared resources or calling methods that may not be thread-safe.

4. **Asynchronous Method Definition:**
   - Define asynchronous methods with a return type of `Task` or `Task<TResult>` to make them awaitable:
     ```csharp
     async Task<int> GetAnswerToLife() {
         await Task.Delay(5000);
         return 21 * 2;
     }
     ```

5. **Awaiting Tasks:**
   - Use `await` to pause execution until a task completes, allowing for a more synchronous coding style while still being non-blocking:
     ```csharp
     async Task Go() {
         int answer = await GetAnswerToLife();
         Console.WriteLine(answer);
     }
     ```

6. **TaskCompletionSource:**
   - Use `TaskCompletionSource` for manual task management, allowing you to control when a task is completed or faulted.

7. **Sequential vs. Parallel Execution:**
   - Awaiting tasks creates a sequential flow, while not awaiting allows for parallel execution. For example:
     ```csharp
     var task1 = PrintAnswerToLife();
     var task2 = PrintAnswerToLife();
     await task1; // Ends parallelism here
     await task2;
     ```

8. **Handling Exceptions:**
   - Exceptions in asynchronous methods can be caught using try/catch blocks, ensuring robust error handling.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice implementing asynchronous methods and using `Task.Run` for background operations.
- Familiarize yourself with the Dispatcher for UI updates and the implications of race conditions.
- Understand how to manage task results and exceptions effectively in asynchronous programming.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into asynchronous programming and task management in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on asynchronous programming and task management in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Asynchronous Programming and Task Management**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Incrementing Shared Fields:**
   - In asynchronous methods, you can increment shared fields without locking, but you cannot guarantee the value remains consistent before and after an `await`.
   ```csharp
   async Task<int> GetAnswerToLife() {
       _x++;
       await Task.Delay(5000);
       return 21 * 2;
   }
   ```

2. **Asynchronous Lambda Expressions:**
   - Both named and unnamed methods (lambdas) can be asynchronous when prefixed with `async`. They can be awaited similarly.
   ```csharp
   Func<Task> unnamed = async () => {
       await Task.Delay(1000);
       Console.WriteLine("Foo");
   };
   await unnamed();
   ```

3. **Event Handlers:**
   - Asynchronous lambda expressions can be used for event handlers, providing a more concise syntax compared to traditional methods.
   ```csharp
   myButton.Click += async (sender, args) => {
       await Task.Delay(1000);
       myButton.Content = "Done";
   };
   ```

4. **WinRT Asynchronous Methods:**
   - In WinRT, `IAsyncAction` and `IAsyncOperation<TResult>` are used instead of `Task` and `Task<TResult>`. You can convert them to tasks using the `AsTask` extension method.
   ```csharp
   Task<StorageFile> fileTask = KnownFolders.DocumentsLibrary.CreateFileAsync("test.txt").AsTask();
   ```

5. **Synchronization Context:**
   - The synchronization context plays a crucial role in managing continuations and exception handling in asynchronous methods. It ensures that exceptions in void-returning async methods are posted to the context.
   ```csharp
   async void ButtonClick(object sender, RoutedEventArgs args) {
       await Task.Delay(1000);
       throw new Exception("Will this be ignored?");
   }
   ```

6. **OperationStarted and OperationCompleted:**
   - When a synchronization context is present, void-returning async functions call `OperationStarted` upon entry and `OperationCompleted` upon exit, which is useful for custom synchronization contexts.

7. **Synchronous Completion:**
   - Asynchronous methods can complete synchronously if no `await` is encountered, returning an already-completed task. This is efficient for cached results.
   ```csharp
   async Task<string> GetWebPageAsync(string uri) {
       if (_cache.TryGetValue(uri, out string html)) return html;
       return _cache[uri] = await new WebClient().DownloadStringTaskAsync(uri);
   }
   ```

8. **Thread-Safe Caching:**
   - To avoid redundant downloads, use a cache of tasks (`Task<string>`) instead of strings. This allows multiple calls to the same URI to wait for the ongoing request.
   ```csharp
   static Dictionary<string, Task<string>> _cache = new Dictionary<string, Task<string>>();
   Task<string> GetWebPageAsync(string uri) {
       if (_cache.TryGetValue(uri, out Task<string> downloadTask)) return downloadTask;
       return _cache[uri] = new WebClient().DownloadStringTaskAsync(uri);
   }
   ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice writing asynchronous methods and using lambda expressions for event handling.
- Understand the implications of synchronization contexts and how they affect exception handling.
- Familiarize yourself with caching strategies using tasks to improve efficiency in asynchronous programming.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into asynchronous programming and task management in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on asynchronous programming and task management in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Asynchronous Programming, Cancellation, and Progress Reporting**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Avoiding Excessive Bouncing:**
   - Use `ConfigureAwait(false)` to prevent continuations from bouncing back to the UI message loop, reducing overhead and improving performance, especially in loops.

2. **Cancellation Tokens:**
   - Implement cancellation in asynchronous methods using `CancellationToken`. Create a `CancellationTokenSource` to manage cancellation requests.
   - Example of a cancellable method:
     ```csharp
     async Task Foo(CancellationToken cancellationToken) {
         for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) {
             Console.WriteLine(i);
             await Task.Delay(1000, cancellationToken);
         }
     }
     ```
   - The `CancellationToken` allows for immediate task cancellation without waiting for the delay to complete.

3. **Progress Reporting:**
   - Use `IProgress<T>` and `Progress<T>` to report progress safely in UI applications. This avoids thread-safety issues by marshaling updates through the synchronization context.
   - Example of a method that reports progress:
     ```csharp
     Task Foo(IProgress<int> onProgressPercentChanged) {
         return Task.Run(() => {
             for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
                 if (i % 10 == 0) onProgressPercentChanged.Report(i / 10);
             }
         });
     }
     ```

4. **Handling Cancellation and Progress in Asynchronous Methods:**
   - Asynchronous methods can accept both a `CancellationToken` and an `IProgress<T>` parameter to manage cancellation and report progress simultaneously.

5. **WinRT Asynchronous Methods:**
   - WinRT methods use a different protocol for cancellation and progress reporting, but the `AsTask` extension method allows for easier integration with .NET's `IProgress<T>`.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using `ConfigureAwait(false)` to optimize asynchronous methods.
- Familiarize yourself with implementing cancellation using `CancellationToken` and `CancellationTokenSource`.
- Understand how to report progress using `IProgress<T>` and ensure thread safety in UI applications.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into asynchronous programming, cancellation, and progress reporting in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on asynchronous programming, cancellation, and progress reporting in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Task-Based Asynchronous Pattern (TAP) and Task Combinators**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Task-Based Asynchronous Pattern (TAP):**
   - TAP methods return a "hot" `Task` or `Task<TResult>`, have an "Async" suffix, and support cancellation tokens and progress reporting.
   - They quickly return to the caller and do not block threads for I/O-bound operations.

2. **Task Combinators:**
   - Task combinators allow for combining multiple tasks without concern for their specific implementations.
   - Key combinators include:
     - **Task.WhenAny**: Returns a task that completes when any of the provided tasks complete.
     - **Task.WhenAll**: Returns a task that completes when all provided tasks complete, aggregating exceptions if any tasks fault.

3. **Using WhenAny:**
   - Example of using `Task.WhenAny` to determine which task completes first:
     ```csharp
     Task<int> winningTask = await Task.WhenAny(Delay1(), Delay2(), Delay3());
     Console.WriteLine(await winningTask); // Outputs the result of the winning task
     ```

4. **Using WhenAll:**
   - Example of using `Task.WhenAll` to wait for multiple tasks:
     ```csharp
     await Task.WhenAll(Delay1(), Delay2(), Delay3());
     ```
   - If any task faults, exceptions are combined into an `AggregateException`.

5. **Custom Task Combinators:**
   - You can create custom combinators, such as:
     - **WithTimeout**: Awaits a task with a specified timeout.
     - **WithCancellation**: Allows a task to be canceled using a `CancellationToken`.

6. **Obsolete Patterns:**
   - The Asynchronous Programming Model (APM) is an older pattern using "Begin" and "End" methods, which is less common now due to the dominance of TAP.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice implementing TAP methods and understand their structure and usage.
- Familiarize yourself with task combinators and how to use them effectively in asynchronous programming.
- Explore creating custom combinators to handle specific asynchronous scenarios.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into TAP and task combinators in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on the Task-Based Asynchronous Pattern and task combinators in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Asynchronous Programming and Task Management**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **TaskCompletionSource and FromAsync:**
   - `TaskCompletionSource` is used to create a task that signals when an APM operation completes or faults.
   - The `FromAsync` method requires a delegate for the `BeginXXX` method, a delegate for the `EndXXX` method, and additional arguments. It supports various asynchronous method signatures in .NET.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Task<int> readChunk = Task<int>.Factory.FromAsync(stream.BeginRead, stream.EndRead, buffer, 0, 1000, null);
     ```

2. **Asynchronous Delegates:**
   - Asynchronous delegates can be called using APM-style `BeginInvoke` and `EndInvoke` methods, but they are less efficient compared to tasks.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Func<string> foo = () => { Thread.Sleep(1000); return "foo"; };
     foo.BeginInvoke(asyncResult => Console.WriteLine(foo.EndInvoke(asyncResult)), null);
     ```

3. **Event-Based Asynchronous Pattern (EAP):**
   - Introduced in .NET Framework 2.0, EAP simplifies asynchronous programming, especially in UI scenarios, but requires more boilerplate code.
   - Example methods include `DownloadDataAsync` and events like `DownloadDataCompleted` for handling completion and progress reporting.

4. **BackgroundWorker:**
   - `BackgroundWorker` provides a straightforward implementation of EAP, allowing for worker threads and progress reporting without manual synchronization context management.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     var worker = new BackgroundWorker { WorkerSupportsCancellation = true };
     worker.DoWork += (sender, args) => { /* Work on a separate thread */ };
     worker.RunWorkerCompleted += (sender, args) => { /* Update UI on completion */ };
     worker.RunWorkerAsync();
     ```

5. **Stream Architecture:**
   - The .NET stream architecture consists of backing store streams (e.g., `FileStream`, `NetworkStream`) and decorator streams (e.g., `DeflateStream`, `CryptoStream`).
   - Streams handle data serially, allowing for efficient memory usage regardless of the backing store size.

6. **Stream Class Members:**
   - The abstract `Stream` class defines methods for reading, writing, seeking, and managing timeouts. Asynchronous versions of these methods are available from .NET Framework 4.5.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using `TaskCompletionSource` and `FromAsync` for managing asynchronous operations.
- Familiarize yourself with the Event-Based Asynchronous Pattern and how to implement it using `BackgroundWorker`.
- Understand the stream architecture and how to work with different types of streams in .NET.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into asynchronous programming, task management, and stream handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on asynchronous programming, task management, and stream architecture in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Streams and I/O**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **FileStream Basics**:
   - Use `FileStream` to read, write, and seek in files. Check properties like `CanRead`, `CanWrite`, and `CanSeek` to determine stream capabilities.
   - Example of creating and using a `FileStream`:
     ```csharp
     using (Stream s = new FileStream("test.txt", FileMode.Create)) {
         s.WriteByte(101);
         s.WriteByte(102);
         byte[] block = { 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 };
         s.Write(block, 0, block.Length);
         s.Position = 0;
         Console.WriteLine(s.ReadByte()); // Outputs 101
     }
     ```

2. **Asynchronous Operations**:
   - Use `ReadAsync` and `WriteAsync` for non-blocking I/O operations, improving application responsiveness.
   - Example of an asynchronous method:
     ```csharp
     async static void AsyncDemo() {
         using (Stream s = new FileStream("test.txt", FileMode.Create)) {
             byte[] block = { 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 };
             await s.WriteAsync(block, 0, block.Length);
             s.Position = 0;
             Console.WriteLine(await s.ReadAsync(block, 0, block.Length)); // Outputs 5
         }
     }
     ```

3. **Reading and Writing Data**:
   - Streams can be read-only, write-only, or both. Use `Read` to fill a byte array and check for the end of the stream when it returns 0.
   - Example of reading a stream correctly:
     ```csharp
     byte[] data = new byte[1000];
     int bytesRead = 0, chunkSize;
     while (bytesRead < data.Length && (chunkSize = s.Read(data, bytesRead, data.Length - bytesRead)) > 0) {
         bytesRead += chunkSize;
     }
     ```

4. **Seeking in Streams**:
   - Seekable streams allow you to change the position of reading/writing. Use `Position` and `Seek` methods to navigate through the stream.
   - Non-seekable streams require reading through to determine length.

5. **Closing and Flushing Streams**:
   - Always dispose of streams to release resources. Use `using` statements to ensure proper disposal.
   - The `Flush` method forces buffered data to be written to the backing store.

6. **Timeouts and Thread Safety**:
   - Some streams support read/write timeouts. Handle timeouts by catching exceptions.
   - Streams are generally not thread-safe. Use `Stream.Synchronized` to create a thread-safe wrapper around a stream.

7. **FileStream Construction**:
   - Use static methods like `File.OpenRead`, `File.OpenWrite`, and `File.Create` for easy instantiation of `FileStream`.
   - Example of opening a file for read/write:
     ```csharp
     var fs = new FileStream("readwrite.tmp", FileMode.Open);
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using `FileStream` for various file operations, including reading, writing, and seeking.
- Familiarize yourself with asynchronous I/O operations to enhance application performance.
- Understand the importance of proper stream disposal and thread safety in concurrent applications.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into streams and I/O in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on streams and I/O in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Streams and I/O**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **File Class Shortcut Methods**:
   - Use static methods for quick file operations:
     - `File.ReadAllText`, `File.ReadAllLines`, `File.ReadAllBytes` for reading.
     - `File.WriteAllText`, `File.WriteAllLines`, `File.WriteAllBytes`, `File.AppendAllText` for writing.
   - `File.ReadLines` returns an `IEnumerable<string>`, allowing for efficient line-by-line processing.

2. **File Paths**:
   - File names can be absolute or relative. Use `AppDomain.CurrentDomain.BaseDirectory` for application-relative paths.
   - Use `Path.Combine` to construct file paths safely.

3. **FileMode and FileAccess**:
   - Specify `FileMode` when creating a `FileStream` to control file access (e.g., `FileMode.Create`, `FileMode.Open`).
   - Use `FileAccess` to define read/write permissions.

4. **Advanced FileStream Features**:
   - Customize `FileStream` with options like `FileShare`, buffer size, and `FileOptions` for encryption or deletion on close.
   - Use `Lock` and `Unlock` methods to manage access to file sections.

5. **MemoryStream**:
   - `MemoryStream` allows for in-memory data manipulation. Use `ToArray` to convert to a byte array.
   - Closing a `MemoryStream` is optional, but it prevents further read/write operations.

6. **PipeStream**:
   - Introduced for interprocess communication (IPC) using anonymous and named pipes.
   - Named pipes allow two-way communication between processes, while anonymous pipes are one-way.

7. **Named Pipes**:
   - Use `NamedPipeServerStream` and `NamedPipeClientStream` for server-client communication.
   - Ensure both parties agree on message lengths and protocols for data transmission.

8. **Message Transmission**:
   - Enable message transmission mode in pipes to determine when a message is complete using the `IsMessageComplete` property.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using the `File` class for various file operations and understand the implications of different `FileMode` and `FileAccess` settings.
- Familiarize yourself with `MemoryStream` and its use cases, especially for temporary data storage.
- Understand how to implement IPC using `PipeStream` and the protocols required for communication between processes.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into streams and I/O in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on streams and I/O in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Pipes, Buffered Streams, and Stream Adapters**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Named Pipes**:
   - Use `NamedPipeServerStream` and `NamedPipeClientStream` for two-way communication between processes. Activate message transmission mode with `PipeTransmissionMode.Message`.
   - Example server code:
     ```csharp
     using (var server = new NamedPipeServerStream("pipedream", PipeDirection.InOut, 1, PipeTransmissionMode.Message)) {
         server.WaitForConnection();
         byte[] msg = Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes("Hello");
         server.Write(msg, 0, msg.Length);
         Console.WriteLine(Encoding.UTF8.GetString(ReadMessage(server)));
     }
     ```
   - Example client code:
     ```csharp
     using (var client = new NamedPipeClientStream("pipedream")) {
         client.Connect();
         client.ReadMode = PipeTransmissionMode.Message;
         Console.WriteLine(Encoding.UTF8.GetString(ReadMessage(client)));
         byte[] msg = Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes("Hello right back!");
         client.Write(msg, 0, msg.Length);
     }
     ```

2. **Anonymous Pipes**:
   - Provide one-way communication between a parent and child process. The server creates an `AnonymousPipeServerStream`, and the client uses `AnonymousPipeClientStream`.
   - Example server code:
     ```csharp
     using (var tx = new AnonymousPipeServerStream(PipeDirection.Out, HandleInheritability.Inheritable))
     using (var rx = new AnonymousPipeServerStream(PipeDirection.In, HandleInheritability.Inheritable)) {
         string txID = tx.GetClientHandleAsString();
         string rxID = rx.GetClientHandleAsString();
         Process.Start(new ProcessStartInfo(clientExe, txID + " " + rxID));
         tx.DisposeLocalCopyOfClientHandle();
         rx.DisposeLocalCopyOfClientHandle();
         tx.WriteByte(100);
         Console.WriteLine("Server received: " + rx.ReadByte());
     }
     ```

3. **Buffered Streams**:
   - `BufferedStream` wraps another stream to improve performance by reducing the number of I/O operations. It buffers data, allowing for more efficient reading/writing.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     using (FileStream fs = File.OpenRead("myFile.bin"))
     using (BufferedStream bs = new BufferedStream(fs, 20000)) {
         bs.ReadByte();
         Console.WriteLine(fs.Position); // Shows position after reading
     }
     ```

4. **Stream Adapters**:
   - Adapters like `StreamReader`, `StreamWriter`, `BinaryReader`, and `BinaryWriter` facilitate reading and writing different data types (e.g., strings, integers) from streams.
   - `TextReader` and `TextWriter` are used for character data, while `BinaryReader` and `BinaryWriter` handle primitive types.

5. **TextReader and TextWriter**:
   - `TextReader` provides methods for reading characters and strings, while `TextWriter` offers methods for writing characters and strings.
   - Key methods include `ReadLine`, `ReadToEnd`, `Write`, and `WriteLine`.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice implementing named and anonymous pipes for interprocess communication.
- Familiarize yourself with using `BufferedStream` to enhance I/O performance.
- Understand how to use stream adapters for reading and writing various data types.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into streams, pipes, and I/O in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on pipes, buffered streams, and stream adapters in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: StreamReader, StreamWriter, and Stream Adapters**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **StreamReader and StreamWriter:**
   - Use `StreamWriter` to write text to files and `StreamReader` to read text from files.
   - Example of writing and reading lines:
     ```csharp
     using (FileStream fs = File.Create("test.txt"))
     using (TextWriter writer = new StreamWriter(fs)) {
         writer.WriteLine("Line1");
         writer.WriteLine("Line2");
     }
     using (FileStream fs = File.OpenRead("test.txt"))
     using (TextReader reader = new StreamReader(fs)) {
         Console.WriteLine(reader.ReadLine()); // Outputs: Line1
         Console.WriteLine(reader.ReadLine()); // Outputs: Line2
     }
     ```

2. **File Class Shortcuts:**
   - The `File` class provides static methods like `CreateText`, `AppendText`, and `OpenText` for easier file operations.
   - Example of appending text:
     ```csharp
     using (TextWriter writer = File.AppendText("test.txt")) {
         writer.WriteLine("Line3");
     }
     ```

3. **Reading and Writing Other Types:**
   - You can write various data types using `StreamWriter`, but they are converted to strings. When reading, you must parse them back to their original types.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     using (TextWriter w = File.CreateText("data.txt")) {
         w.WriteLine(123); // Writes "123"
         w.WriteLine(true); // Writes "True"
     }
     using (TextReader r = File.OpenText("data.txt")) {
         int myInt = int.Parse(r.ReadLine()); // myInt == 123
         bool yes = bool.Parse(r.ReadLine()); // yes == true
     }
     ```

4. **Character Encodings:**
   - `StreamReader` and `StreamWriter` convert between characters and bytes using an `Encoding` class. The default is UTF-8, but you can specify others.
   - Example of specifying UTF-8 without a BOM:
     ```csharp
     var encoding = new UTF8Encoding(encoderShouldEmitUTF8Identifier: false, throwOnInvalidBytes: true);
     ```

5. **StringReader and StringWriter:**
   - These classes use strings or `StringBuilder` as the data source, eliminating the need for byte translation.
   - Example of using `StringReader` with `XmlReader`:
     ```csharp
     XmlReader r = XmlReader.Create(new StringReader(myString));
     ```

6. **BinaryReader and BinaryWriter:**
   - These classes read and write native data types efficiently, using the same memory representation.
   - Example of saving/loading a custom type:
     ```csharp
     public void SaveData(Stream s) {
         var w = new BinaryWriter(s);
         w.Write(Name);
         w.Write(Age);
         w.Write(Height);
         w.Flush();
     }
     public void LoadData(Stream s) {
         var r = new BinaryReader(s);
         Name = r.ReadString();
         Age = r.ReadInt32();
         Height = r.ReadDouble();
     }
     ```

7. **Closing and Disposing Stream Adapters:**
   - Properly manage the lifecycle of stream adapters by closing them in the correct order to avoid data loss.
   - Use nested `using` statements to ensure that both the adapter and the underlying stream are disposed of correctly.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using `StreamReader` and `StreamWriter` for file I/O operations.
- Familiarize yourself with character encodings and their implications for reading/writing text.
- Understand how to use binary adapters for efficient data serialization and deserialization.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into streams and I/O in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on stream handling and I/O operations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Stream Adapters, Compression, and File Operations**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Stream Adapters:**
   - Stream adapters like `StreamWriter` and `StreamReader` can be optionally disposable. You can choose not to dispose of them if you want to keep the underlying stream open.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     using (FileStream fs = new FileStream("test.txt", FileMode.Create))
     {
         StreamWriter writer = new StreamWriter(fs);
         writer.WriteLine("Hello");
         writer.Flush();
         fs.Position = 0;
         Console.WriteLine(fs.ReadByte());
     }
     ```
   - From .NET Framework 4.5, you can use constructors that keep the underlying stream open after disposal.

2. **Compression Streams:**
   - `DeflateStream` and `GZipStream` are used for compressing and decompressing data. GZipStream includes additional error-checking protocols.
   - Example of compressing data:
     ```csharp
     using (Stream s = File.Create("compressed.bin"))
     using (Stream ds = new DeflateStream(s, CompressionMode.Compress))
     {
         for (byte i = 0; i < 100; i++)
             ds.WriteByte(i);
     }
     ```
   - Compression works best with repetitive data, such as text files.

3. **In-Memory Compression:**
   - You can compress data in memory using `MemoryStream`:
     ```csharp
     byte[] data = new byte[1000];
     var ms = new MemoryStream();
     using (Stream ds = new DeflateStream(ms, CompressionMode.Compress))
         ds.Write(data, 0, data.Length);
     byte[] compressed = ms.ToArray();
     ```

4. **Working with ZIP Files:**
   - The `ZipArchive` and `ZipFile` classes allow for creating and extracting ZIP files, supporting multiple files in a single archive.
   - Example of creating a ZIP file:
     ```csharp
     ZipFile.CreateFromDirectory(@"d:\MyFolder", @"d:\compressed.zip");
     ```
   - You can read entries from a ZIP file and manipulate them using methods like `CreateEntry` and `ExtractToFile`.

5. **File and Directory Operations:**
   - The `File` and `Directory` classes provide static methods for file and directory operations, while `FileInfo` and `DirectoryInfo` offer instance methods.
   - Use the `Path` class for manipulating file and directory paths.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using stream adapters and understand their disposal behavior.
- Familiarize yourself with compression streams and their use cases.
- Learn how to work with ZIP files and perform file operations using both static and instance methods.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into streams, compression, and file operations in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on stream handling, compression, and file operations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: File and Directory Operations**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **File Class**:
   - The `File` class provides static methods for file operations, including:
     - `Exists(string path)`: Checks if a file exists.
     - `Delete(string path)`: Deletes a file.
     - `Copy(string sourceFileName, string destFileName)`: Copies a file.
     - `Move(string sourceFileName, string destFileName)`: Moves a file.
     - `GetAttributes(string path)`: Retrieves file attributes.
     - `SetAttributes(string path, FileAttributes fileAttributes)`: Sets file attributes.
     - `Encrypt(string path)` and `Decrypt(string path)`: Manage file encryption.
     - `GetCreationTime(string path)`, `GetLastAccessTime(string path)`, and `GetLastWriteTime(string path)`: Retrieve file timestamps.

2. **File Attributes**:
   - Attributes like `ReadOnly`, `Hidden`, `Encrypted`, and `Compressed` can be managed using `GetAttributes` and `SetAttributes`.
   - Use bitwise operations to toggle attributes without affecting others.

3. **File Security**:
   - Use `GetAccessControl` and `SetAccessControl` to manage file permissions via `FileSecurity` objects.
   - Example: Listing existing permissions and adding new access rules.

4. **Directory Class**:
   - The `Directory` class provides methods for directory operations, including:
     - `Exists(string path)`: Checks if a directory exists.
     - `CreateDirectory(string path)`: Creates a new directory.
     - `GetFiles(string path)`: Retrieves files in a directory.
     - `EnumerateFiles(string path)`: Lazily enumerates files, improving efficiency.

5. **DirectoryInfo and FileInfo**:
   - Use `FileInfo` and `DirectoryInfo` classes for object-oriented file and directory management, allowing for method chaining and easier manipulation of file properties.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using the `File` and `Directory` classes for various file and directory operations.
- Familiarize yourself with file attributes and security management.
- Understand the benefits of using `FileInfo` and `DirectoryInfo` for more complex file operations.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into file and directory operations in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on file and directory operations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: File and Directory Operations**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **FileInfo Class:**
   - `FileInfo` provides instance methods for file operations, including properties like `Exists`, `Extension`, `Length`, and methods for file manipulation (e.g., `MoveTo`, `Encrypt`).
   - Example usage:
     ```csharp
     FileInfo fi = new FileInfo(@"c:\temp\FileInfo.txt");
     using (TextWriter w = fi.CreateText()) w.Write("Some text");
     fi.Refresh(); // Check if file exists
     ```

2. **DirectoryInfo Class:**
   - `DirectoryInfo` allows for directory operations, such as creating subdirectories and enumerating files.
   - Example of enumerating files:
     ```csharp
     DirectoryInfo di = new DirectoryInfo(@"e:\photos");
     foreach (FileInfo fi in di.GetFiles("*.jpg")) Console.WriteLine(fi.Name);
     ```

3. **Path Class:**
   - The `Path` class provides methods for manipulating file paths, such as `Combine`, `GetDirectoryName`, and `GetFileName`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     string path = @"c:\mydir\myfile.txt";
     Console.WriteLine(Path.GetFileName(path)); // Outputs: myfile.txt
     ```

4. **Temporary Files:**
   - Use `Path.GetTempPath()`, `Path.GetRandomFileName()`, and `Path.GetTempFileName()` for managing temporary files.
   - Note: `GetTempFileName` creates a zero-byte file that must be deleted after use.

5. **Special Folders:**
   - Use `Environment.GetFolderPath` to access special directories like My Documents and Application Data.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     string myDocPath = Environment.GetFolderPath(Environment.SpecialFolder.MyDocuments);
     ```

6. **File and Directory Security:**
   - Manage file permissions using `GetAccessControl` and `SetAccessControl` to ensure proper access rights.
   - Example of assigning full control to a folder:
     ```csharp
     public void AssignUsersFullControlToFolder(string path) { /* Implementation */ }
     ```

7. **DriveInfo Class:**
   - Use `DriveInfo` to query information about drives, such as total size and available space.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     DriveInfo c = new DriveInfo("C");
     long totalSize = c.TotalSize;
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using `FileInfo` and `DirectoryInfo` for file and directory management.
- Familiarize yourself with the `Path` class for path manipulation.
- Understand how to manage temporary files and special folders in Windows.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into file and directory operations in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on file and directory operations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: File and Directory Operations, FileSystemWatcher, and UWP File I/O**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Drive Information:**
   - Use the `DriveInfo` class to access properties of drives, such as `Name`, `DriveType`, `RootDirectory`, `VolumeLabel`, and `DriveFormat`. The `GetDrives` method returns all mapped drives, including removable and network drives.

2. **FileSystemWatcher:**
   - The `FileSystemWatcher` class monitors directories for changes (creation, modification, deletion, renaming). It raises events on a separate thread, so exception handling is crucial to prevent application crashes.
   - Example setup:
     ```csharp
     static void Watch(string path, string filter, bool includeSubDirs) {
         using (var watcher = new FileSystemWatcher(path, filter)) {
             watcher.Created += FileCreatedChangedDeleted;
             watcher.Changed += FileCreatedChangedDeleted;
             watcher.Deleted += FileCreatedChangedDeleted;
             watcher.Renamed += FileRenamed;
             watcher.Error += FileError;
             watcher.IncludeSubdirectories = includeSubDirs;
             watcher.EnableRaisingEvents = true;
         }
     }
     ```

3. **UWP File I/O:**
   - UWP applications have restricted access to files and directories. Use `StorageFolder` and `StorageFile` classes for file operations, with methods like `GetFilesAsync`, `CreateFileAsync`, and `OpenStreamForReadAsync`.
   - Example of creating and writing to a file:
     ```csharp
     StorageFolder docsFolder = KnownFolders.DocumentsLibrary;
     StorageFile file = await docsFolder.CreateFileAsync("test.txt", CreationCollisionOption.ReplaceExisting);
     using (Stream stream = await file.OpenStreamForWriteAsync())
     using (StreamWriter writer = new StreamWriter(stream))
         await writer.WriteLineAsync("This is a test");
     ```

4. **Memory-Mapped Files:**
   - Memory-mapped files allow efficient random access to file data and enable memory sharing between processes. They are faster than `FileStream` for random I/O operations.
   - To create a memory-mapped file, obtain a `FileStream`, instantiate a `MemoryMappedFile`, and call `CreateViewAccessor`.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using `DriveInfo` and `FileSystemWatcher` for monitoring file system changes.
- Familiarize yourself with UWP file access restrictions and how to use `StorageFolder` and `StorageFile`.
- Understand the benefits of memory-mapped files for efficient data access and inter-process communication.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into file and directory operations, file system monitoring, and UWP file I/O. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on file and directory operations, file system monitoring, and UWP file handling in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Memory-Mapped Files and Networking**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Memory-Mapped Files:**
   - Memory-mapped files allow for efficient random access to file data and can be used for inter-process communication.
   - Example of creating and using a memory-mapped file:
     ```csharp
     File.WriteAllBytes("long.bin", new byte[1000000]);
     using (MemoryMappedFile mmf = MemoryMappedFile.CreateFromFile("long.bin"))
     using (MemoryMappedViewAccessor accessor = mmf.CreateViewAccessor())
     {
         accessor.Write(500000, (byte)77);
         Console.WriteLine(accessor.ReadByte(500000)); // Outputs: 77
     }
     ```
   - Shared memory can be created using `MemoryMappedFile.CreateNew` and accessed by other processes with `MemoryMappedFile.OpenExisting`.

2. **Working with View Accessors:**
   - View accessors allow reading and writing values at random positions in memory-mapped files.
   - You can read/write simple types, structures, and arrays, but not reference types.
   - Example of writing a string:
     ```csharp
     byte[] data = Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes("This is a test");
     accessor.Write(0, data.Length);
     accessor.WriteArray(4, data, 0, data.Length);
     ```

3. **Performance Considerations:**
   - Directly accessing unmanaged memory via pointers can significantly improve performance, especially for large structures.
   - Example of using pointers:
     ```csharp
     unsafe
     {
         byte* pointer = null;
         accessor.SafeMemoryMappedViewHandle.AcquirePointer(ref pointer);
         int* intPointer = (int*)pointer;
         Console.WriteLine(*intPointer); // Outputs: 123
     }
     ```

4. **Isolated Storage:**
   - Isolated storage provides a unique local storage area for each .NET application, useful for applications with restricted filesystem access.

5. **Networking Overview:**
   - The .NET Framework provides classes for network communication, including `WebClient`, `HttpClient`, `TcpClient`, and `SmtpClient`.
   - Networking types are organized into transport and application layers, with HTTP being a key protocol for web communication.

6. **Network Protocols:**
   - Common protocols include:
     - **HTTP**: For web page retrieval and web services.
     - **FTP**: For file transfer.
     - **SMTP**: For sending emails.
     - **TCP/UDP**: For transport layer communication.

7. **Addressing Systems:**
   - The Internet uses IPv4 (32-bit addresses) and IPv6 for addressing devices.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice creating and using memory-mapped files and view accessors for efficient data access.
- Familiarize yourself with isolated storage and its use cases.
- Understand the various networking classes and protocols available in .NET for building networked applications.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into memory-mapped files and networking in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on memory-mapped files and networking in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Networking and URIs**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **IP Addresses:**
   - IPv4 addresses are represented as four dot-separated decimals (e.g., 101.102.103.104). IPv6 addresses are 128-bit and formatted in hexadecimal with colons (e.g., [3EA0:FFFF:198A:E4A3:4FF2:54fA:41BC:8D31]).
   - The `IPAddress` class in the `System.Net` namespace can be instantiated using a byte array or a string. It supports both IPv4 and IPv6.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     IPAddress a1 = new IPAddress(new byte[] { 101, 102, 103, 104 });
     IPAddress a2 = IPAddress.Parse("101.102.103.104");
     ```

2. **Ports and Endpoints:**
   - Each IP address can have up to 65,535 ports, allowing multiple applications to run simultaneously. Common ports include 80 (HTTP) and 25 (SMTP).
   - The `IPEndPoint` class represents an IP address and port combination.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     IPEndPoint ep = new IPEndPoint(a1, 222);
     ```

3. **URIs:**
   - A URI (Uniform Resource Identifier) describes a resource on the Internet or a LAN. The `Uri` class in the `System` namespace helps parse and validate URIs.
   - URIs can be absolute or relative, and the `Uri` class provides properties to access different components (scheme, authority, path).
   - Example of creating a URI:
     ```csharp
     Uri uri = new Uri("http://www.example.com");
     ```

4. **URI Properties:**
   - Properties like `IsLoopback`, `IsFile`, and `IsUnc` help determine the type of URI.
   - The `UriBuilder` class allows modification of URI components.

5. **Client-Side Networking:**
   - `WebRequest` and `WebResponse` are base classes for HTTP and FTP operations, while `WebClient` provides a simpler interface for downloading and uploading data.
   - `HttpClient` is a more advanced class for working with HTTP-based APIs and supports asynchronous operations.
   - Example of using `WebClient`:
     ```csharp
     using (WebClient client = new WebClient())
     {
         string data = client.DownloadString("http://www.example.com");
     }
     ```

6. **Concurrency in HTTP Requests:**
   - The default connection limit for HTTP requests can be increased using `ServicePointManager.DefaultConnectionLimit` to allow more concurrent requests.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice working with `IPAddress`, `IPEndPoint`, and `Uri` classes to understand networking fundamentals.
- Familiarize yourself with `WebClient` and `HttpClient` for making HTTP requests and handling responses.
- Understand the significance of ports and how to manage them in networked applications.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into networking and URI handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on networking concepts and URI management in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Networking with WebClient, WebRequest, and HttpClient**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **WebClient:**
   - Use `WebClient` for simple web requests. It allows downloading files and supports asynchronous operations with methods like `DownloadFileTaskAsync`.
   - Example of downloading a file:
     ```csharp
     WebClient wc = new WebClient { Proxy = null };
     await wc.DownloadFileTaskAsync("http://oreilly.com", "webpage.htm");
     ```
   - For progress reporting, handle `DownloadProgressChanged` events, and for cancellation, use `CancelAsync`.

2. **WebRequest and WebResponse:**
   - `WebRequest` provides more flexibility than `WebClient`. To use it:
     1. Create a request with `WebRequest.Create`.
     2. Set properties like `Proxy` and `Credentials`.
     3. Use `GetRequestStream` to upload data or `GetResponseStream` to download data.
   - Example of downloading a webpage:
     ```csharp
     WebRequest req = WebRequest.Create("http://www.albahari.com/nutshell/code.html");
     using (WebResponse res = req.GetResponse())
     using (Stream rs = res.GetResponseStream())
     using (FileStream fs = File.Create("code.html"))
         rs.CopyTo(fs);
     ```

3. **HttpClient:**
   - Introduced in .NET Framework 4.5, `HttpClient` is designed for HTTP-based APIs and supports concurrent requests.
   - Example of using `HttpClient`:
     ```csharp
     var client = new HttpClient();
     string html = await client.GetStringAsync("http://linqpad.net");
     ```
   - `HttpClient` allows for custom message handlers and is more efficient when reused for multiple requests.

4. **Asynchronous Operations:**
   - All I/O-bound methods in `HttpClient` are asynchronous. Use `GetAsync` for general requests and handle responses with `HttpResponseMessage`.
   - Example of handling a response:
     ```csharp
     HttpResponseMessage response = await client.GetAsync("http://...");
     response.EnsureSuccessStatusCode();
     string html = await response.Content.ReadAsStringAsync();
     ```

5. **Timeouts and Error Handling:**
   - `WebRequest` has a `Timeout` property, and a timeout results in a `WebException`.
   - `HttpClient` also has a `Timeout` property, and unsuccessful status codes do not throw exceptions unless `EnsureSuccessStatusCode` is called.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using `WebClient`, `WebRequest`, and `HttpClient` for various web operations.
- Familiarize yourself with handling asynchronous operations and error management in network requests.
- Understand the differences between these classes and when to use each based on the complexity of the task.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into networking in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on networking concepts and classes in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: HttpClient and Networking**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **HttpRequestMessage:**
   - Customize HTTP requests by instantiating `HttpRequestMessage`, allowing you to set headers and content types for uploads.
   - Content can be assigned using the `HttpContent` subclasses like `ByteArrayContent`, `StringContent`, `FormUrlEncodedContent`, and `StreamContent`.

2. **HttpMessageHandler:**
   - `HttpMessageHandler` is an abstract class that defines how requests are sent. You can subclass it to create custom handlers for unit testing or to extend functionality.
   - Example of a mock handler for unit testing:
     ```csharp
     class MockHandler : HttpMessageHandler {
         // Implementation for generating mock responses
     }
     ```

3. **DelegatingHandler:**
   - Subclass `DelegatingHandler` to create a chain of handlers for tasks like logging, authentication, or compression.
   - Example of a logging handler:
     ```csharp
     class LoggingHandler : DelegatingHandler {
         // Implementation for logging requests and responses
     }
     ```

4. **Proxy Configuration:**
   - Use `WebProxy` to route requests through a proxy server, which can enhance security and manage access.
   - Example of setting up a proxy:
     ```csharp
     WebProxy p = new WebProxy("192.178.10.49", 808);
     p.Credentials = new NetworkCredential("username", "password");
     ```

5. **Authentication:**
   - Use `NetworkCredential` to provide credentials for HTTP or FTP requests. This works with various authentication protocols, including Basic and Digest.
   - Example of setting credentials:
     ```csharp
     wc.Credentials = new NetworkCredential("username", "password");
     ```

6. **HttpClient Usage:**
   - `HttpClient` is designed for making HTTP requests and supports asynchronous operations. It can be configured with a custom `HttpClientHandler` for proxy and authentication.
   - Example of using `HttpClient`:
     ```csharp
     var client = new HttpClient(new HttpClientHandler { Proxy = p });
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice creating and customizing `HttpRequestMessage` for various HTTP methods.
- Familiarize yourself with creating mock handlers for unit testing and logging requests.
- Understand how to configure proxies and authentication for secure network communication.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into `HttpClient`, networking, and authentication in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on `HttpClient`, networking concepts, and handling requests in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Authentication, Exception Handling, and HTTP Networking**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Authentication Protocols:**
   - Authentication is managed by subclasses of `WebRequest`, such as `FtpWebRequest`, which negotiate compatible protocols (e.g., NTLM, Basic).
   - The `WWW-Authenticate` header in HTTP responses indicates supported authentication methods.
   - Use the `PreAuthenticate` property in `HttpWebRequest` to avoid extra round trips for subsequent requests.

2. **CredentialCache:**
   - A `CredentialCache` allows you to store multiple `NetworkCredential` objects for different authentication protocols and URIs.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     CredentialCache cache = new CredentialCache();
     Uri prefix = new Uri("http://exchange.somedomain.com");
     cache.Add(prefix, "Digest", new NetworkCredential("joe", "passwd"));
     ```

3. **HttpClient Authentication:**
   - With `HttpClient`, you can set the authentication header directly for requests:
     ```csharp
     client.DefaultRequestHeaders.Authorization = new AuthenticationHeaderValue("Basic", Convert.ToBase64String(Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes("username:password")));
     ```

4. **Exception Handling:**
   - Classes like `WebRequest`, `WebResponse`, and `HttpClient` throw `WebException` or `HttpRequestException` for network errors.
   - Use the `Status` property of `WebException` to identify specific errors (e.g., `NameResolutionFailure`, `ProtocolError`).
   - Example of handling exceptions:
     ```csharp
     try {
         string s = wc.DownloadString("http://www.albahari.com/notthere");
     } catch (WebException ex) {
         if (ex.Status == WebExceptionStatus.NameResolutionFailure) {
             Console.WriteLine("Bad domain name");
         }
     }
     ```

5. **Working with HTTP:**
   - You can add custom HTTP headers using `WebClient`, `WebRequest`, and `HttpClient`.
   - Example of adding a custom header:
     ```csharp
     wc.Headers.Add("CustomHeader", "JustPlaying/1.0");
     ```

6. **Query Strings:**
   - Query strings can be appended to URIs to send data to the server.
   - Use `Uri.EscapeDataString` to encode query parameters safely.
   - Example of constructing a query string:
     ```csharp
     string search = Uri.EscapeDataString("(WebClient OR HttpClient)");
     string requestURI = "http://www.google.com/search?q=" + search + "&hl=fr";
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Familiarize yourself with different authentication protocols and how to implement them using `CredentialCache` and `HttpClient`.
- Practice exception handling for network-related errors and understand how to interpret `WebException` statuses.
- Learn how to manipulate HTTP headers and query strings effectively in your requests.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into authentication, exception handling, and HTTP networking in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on authentication, exception handling, and HTTP networking in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Working with HTTP**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Uploading Form Data**:
   - Use `WebClient` to easily upload form data with `UploadValues` method.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     WebClient wc = new WebClient { Proxy = null };
     var data = new NameValueCollection { { "Name", "Joe Albahari" }, { "Company", "O'Reilly" } };
     byte[] result = wc.UploadValues("http://www.albahari.com/EchoPost.aspx", "POST", data);
     Console.WriteLine(Encoding.UTF8.GetString(result));
     ```

2. **Using WebRequest for Form Data**:
   - For more control (e.g., cookies), use `WebRequest`:
     - Set `ContentType` to "application/x-www-form-urlencoded".
     - Build and encode the data string.
     - Write the data to the request stream and get the response.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     var req = WebRequest.Create("http://www.albahari.com/EchoPost.aspx");
     req.Method = "POST";
     req.ContentType = "application/x-www-form-urlencoded";
     string reqString = "Name=Joe+Albahari&Company=O'Reilly";
     byte[] reqData = Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes(reqString);
     req.ContentLength = reqData.Length;
     using (Stream reqStream = req.GetRequestStream()) reqStream.Write(reqData, 0, reqData.Length);
     ```

3. **Using HttpClient for Form Data**:
   - With `HttpClient`, use `FormUrlEncodedContent` for posting data:
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     var client = new HttpClient();
     var values = new FormUrlEncodedContent(new Dictionary<string, string> { { "Name", "Joe Albahari" }, { "Company", "O'Reilly" } });
     var response = await client.PostAsync("http://www.albahari.com/EchoPost.aspx", values);
     response.EnsureSuccessStatusCode();
     Console.WriteLine(await response.Content.ReadAsStringAsync());
     ```

4. **Handling Cookies**:
   - Cookies are name/value pairs sent by the server. Use `CookieContainer` with `WebRequest` or `HttpClientHandler` to manage cookies.
   - Example with `HttpClient`:
     ```csharp
     var handler = new HttpClientHandler { CookieContainer = new CookieContainer() };
     var client = new HttpClient(handler);
     ```

5. **Forms Authentication**:
   - For sites using forms authentication, send a POST request with username and password to log in.
   - Example with `WebRequest`:
     ```csharp
     string loginUri = "http://www.somesite.com/login";
     string reqString = "username=myusername&password=mypassword";
     // Set up request and send data...
     ```

6. **SSL Handling**:
   - Automatically handled with "https" prefix. For invalid certificates, use a custom certificate validator.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     ServicePointManager.ServerCertificateValidationCallback = CertChecker;
     ```

7. **Creating an HTTP Server**:
   - Use `HttpListener` to create a simple HTTP server.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     static void Main() {
         ListenAsync();
     }
     async static void ListenAsync() {
         HttpListener listener = new HttpListener();
         listener.Prefixes.Add("http://localhost:51111/MyApp/");
         listener.Start();
         // Handle requests...
     }
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice uploading form data using `WebClient`, `WebRequest`, and `HttpClient`.
- Familiarize yourself with cookie management and forms authentication.
- Understand SSL handling and how to create a basic HTTP server.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into HTTP networking in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on HTTP operations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: HTTP Server and FTP Operations**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **HttpListener**:
   - Use `HttpListener` to create a simple HTTP server that listens for client requests.
   - Example of handling a request:
     ```csharp
     HttpListenerContext context = await listener.GetContextAsync();
     string msg = "You asked for: " + context.Request.RawUrl;
     context.Response.ContentLength64 = Encoding.UTF8.GetByteCount(msg);
     context.Response.StatusCode = (int)HttpStatusCode.OK;
     using (Stream s = context.Response.OutputStream)
     using (StreamWriter writer = new StreamWriter(s))
         await writer.WriteAsync(msg);
     ```

2. **Asynchronous Processing**:
   - Implement asynchronous request handling to improve scalability. Use `Task.Run` to process requests without blocking the main thread.
   - Example of starting the server:
     ```csharp
     async void Start() {
         _listener.Start();
         while (true) {
             var context = await _listener.GetContextAsync();
             Task.Run(() => ProcessRequestAsync(context));
         }
     }
     ```

3. **File Handling**:
   - Serve files from a specified directory. Check if the requested file exists and respond accordingly.
   - Example of serving a file:
     ```csharp
     string path = Path.Combine(_baseFolder, filename);
     if (!File.Exists(path)) {
         context.Response.StatusCode = (int)HttpStatusCode.NotFound;
     } else {
         context.Response.StatusCode = (int)HttpStatusCode.OK;
         byte[] msg = File.ReadAllBytes(path);
         context.Response.ContentLength64 = msg.Length;
         using (Stream s = context.Response.OutputStream)
             await s.WriteAsync(msg, 0, msg.Length);
     }
     ```

4. **FTP Operations**:
   - Use `WebClient` for simple FTP uploads and downloads.
   - Example of uploading and downloading a file:
     ```csharp
     WebClient wc = new WebClient { Proxy = null };
     wc.Credentials = new NetworkCredential("username", "password");
     wc.UploadString("ftp://ftp.example.com/tempfile.txt", "hello!");
     Console.WriteLine(wc.DownloadString("ftp://ftp.example.com/tempfile.txt"));
     ```

5. **FtpWebRequest**:
   - For more control over FTP operations, use `FtpWebRequest` to execute commands like listing directories, getting file sizes, and renaming files.
   - Example of listing a directory:
     ```csharp
     var req = (FtpWebRequest)WebRequest.Create("ftp://ftp.example.com");
     req.Method = WebRequestMethods.Ftp.ListDirectory;
     using (WebResponse resp = req.GetResponse())
     using (StreamReader reader = new StreamReader(resp.GetResponseStream()))
         Console.WriteLine(reader.ReadToEnd());
     ```

6. **DNS Operations**:
   - Use the `Dns` class to resolve domain names to IP addresses and vice versa.
   - Example of getting IP addresses from a domain:
     ```csharp
     foreach (IPAddress a in Dns.GetHostAddresses("example.com"))
         Console.WriteLine(a.ToString());
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice creating an HTTP server using `HttpListener` and handling requests asynchronously.
- Familiarize yourself with FTP operations using `WebClient` and `FtpWebRequest`.
- Understand how to resolve domain names using the `Dns` class and the implications for network performance.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into HTTP servers, FTP operations, and DNS handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on HTTP server implementation, FTP operations, and DNS resolution in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Networking, SMTP, and TCP Operations**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **DNS Class:**
   - Use `Dns.GetHostAddressesAsync` for asynchronous DNS lookups.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     foreach (IPAddress a in await Dns.GetHostAddressesAsync("albahari.com"))
         Console.WriteLine(a.ToString());
     ```

2. **SmtpClient:**
   - The `SmtpClient` class allows sending emails via SMTP.
   - Basic usage:
     ```csharp
     SmtpClient client = new SmtpClient("mail.myisp.net");
     client.Send("from@domain.com", "to@domain.com", "subject", "body");
     ```
   - For more options, construct a `MailMessage` object to add attachments and set properties like sender, recipients, and priority.

3. **TCP and UDP Protocols:**
   - TCP is connection-oriented and reliable, while UDP is connectionless and has lower overhead.
   - Use `TcpClient` and `TcpListener` for TCP communication.
   - Example of a TCP client:
     ```csharp
     using (TcpClient client = new TcpClient("address", port))
     using (NetworkStream n = client.GetStream())
     {
         // Read and write to the network stream...
     }
     ```

4. **Asynchronous TCP Operations:**
   - Implement asynchronous methods for scalable TCP servers using `TcpListener.AcceptTcpClientAsync`.
   - Example of an asynchronous TCP server:
     ```csharp
     async Task Accept(TcpClient client)
     {
         using (NetworkStream n = client.GetStream())
         {
             byte[] data = new byte[5000];
             int bytesRead = await n.ReadAsync(data, 0, data.Length);
             Array.Reverse(data);
             await n.WriteAsync(data, 0, bytesRead);
         }
     }
     ```

5. **POP3 Mail Retrieval:**
   - POP3 requires direct TCP communication as there is no built-in support in .NET.
   - Example of a simple POP3 conversation:
     ```plaintext
     Client: USER joe
     Server: +OK Password required.
     Client: PASS password
     Server: +OK Logged in.
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using the `Dns` class for DNS lookups and understand asynchronous programming.
- Familiarize yourself with sending emails using `SmtpClient` and constructing `MailMessage` objects.
- Understand TCP and UDP protocols, and practice implementing both synchronous and asynchronous TCP clients and servers.
- Learn the basics of POP3 and how to communicate with a POP3 server over TCP.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into networking, SMTP, and TCP operations in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on networking, email sending, and TCP operations in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Networking and Serialization**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **POP3 Mail Retrieval:**
   - Use TCP to connect to a POP3 server on port 110 to retrieve emails.
   - Implement helper methods to read lines and send commands to the server.
   - Example of retrieving and deleting emails:
     ```csharp
     using (TcpClient client = new TcpClient("mail.isp.com", 110))
     using (NetworkStream n = client.GetStream())
     {
         SendCommand(n, "USER username");
         SendCommand(n, "PASS password");
         SendCommand(n, "LIST");
         // Process message IDs and retrieve messages...
     }
     ```

2. **Windows Runtime TCP:**
   - Use `StreamSocketListener` and `StreamSocket` for TCP communication in Windows Runtime.
   - Example of a simple server that reads a length-prefixed string:
     ```csharp
     async void Server()
     {
         var listener = new StreamSocketListener();
         listener.ConnectionReceived += async (sender, args) => { /* Handle connection */ };
         await listener.BindServiceNameAsync("51111");
     }
     ```

3. **DataReader and DataWriter:**
   - Use `DataReader` for reading data asynchronously and `DataWriter` for writing data to a stream.
   - Example of a client sending a message:
     ```csharp
     async void Client()
     {
         using (var socket = new StreamSocket())
         {
             await socket.ConnectAsync(new HostName("localhost"), "51111");
             var writer = new DataWriter(socket.OutputStream);
             writer.WriteUInt32(length);
             writer.WriteString(message);
             await writer.StoreAsync();
         }
     }
     ```

4. **Serialization Overview:**
   - Serialization converts objects into a format suitable for storage or transmission, while deserialization reconstructs them.
   - Common uses include transmitting objects over networks and storing them in files or databases.

5. **Serialization Engines:**
   - The .NET Framework supports four serialization mechanisms:
     - Data Contract Serializer
     - Binary Serializer
     - XML Serializer (XmlSerializer)
     - IXmlSerializable interface
   - Each engine has different features, automation levels, and performance characteristics.

6. **Comparison of Serialization Engines:**
   - Data Contract Serializer: Flexible, suitable for interoperable messaging, preserves object references.
   - Binary Serializer: High performance, tight type coupling.
   - XML Serializer: Good for XML file manipulation, but less efficient for performance.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice implementing POP3 mail retrieval using TCP and understand the command-response model.
- Familiarize yourself with Windows Runtime networking classes and asynchronous data handling.
- Understand serialization concepts and the differences between serialization engines in .NET.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into networking and serialization in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on networking and serialization in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Serialization Engines**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Data Contract Serializer:**
   - The most versatile serialization engine, used by WCF, ideal for standards-compliant messaging and version tolerance.
   - Supports a data contract model, allowing for decoupling of type details from serialized data structure, enabling deserialization of types that have changed over time.
   - Can be used for general XML reading/writing, but has limitations with attribute storage and unordered elements.

2. **Binary Serializer:**
   - Highly automated and easy to use, suitable for remoting and internal application domain communication.
   - Offers faster performance for full type fidelity but has poor version tolerance due to tight coupling of type structure to serialized format.
   - Not designed for XML output, but provides limited SOAP interoperability.

3. **XML Serializer:**
   - Can only produce XML and is less powerful in restoring complex object graphs (no shared object references).
   - Offers flexibility in XML structure, allowing properties to be serialized as elements or attributes, and provides good version tolerance.

4. **IXmlSerializable Interface:**
   - Allows custom serialization using `XmlReader` and `XmlWriter`, recognized by both XML and data contract serializers for handling complex types.

5. **Formatters:**
   - Formatters shape the output of serialization engines, with options for XML and binary formats.
   - The data contract serializer is geared towards XML messaging, while the binary serializer is optimized for binary output.

6. **Explicit vs. Implicit Serialization:**
   - Explicit serialization is initiated by the developer, allowing choice of engine and formatter.
   - Implicit serialization occurs automatically through frameworks like WCF, which uses the data contract serializer.

7. **Using the Data Contract Serializer:**
   - Steps include choosing between `DataContractSerializer` and `NetDataContractSerializer`, decorating types with `[DataContract]` and members with `[DataMember]`, and calling `WriteObject` or `ReadObject`.

8. **DataContractSerializer vs. NetDataContractSerializer:**
   - The former loosely couples .NET types to data contracts, while the latter tightly couples them, providing full type and assembly names in serialized output.
   - The `DataContractSerializer` is preferred for interoperability and XML file operations, while `NetDataContractSerializer` is suitable for proprietary scenarios.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand the differences between serialization engines and their use cases.
- Practice implementing serialization with the `DataContractSerializer` and `NetDataContractSerializer`.
- Familiarize yourself with the attributes required for serialization and how to explicitly serialize and deserialize objects.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into serialization in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on serialization concepts and engines in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Serialization with DataContractSerializer**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **DataContractSerializer Basics**:
   - The `DataContractSerializer` serializes objects to XML by default, allowing for indentation for readability.
   - You can specify a custom data contract name and namespace using attributes:
     ```csharp
     [DataContract(Name="Candidate", Namespace="http://oreilly.com/nutshell")]
     public class Person { ... }
     ```

2. **DataMember Attributes**:
   - Use `[DataMember]` to control serialization of fields and properties, supporting various data types including primitives, collections, and custom types.
   - You can override member names during serialization:
     ```csharp
     [DataMember(Name="FirstName")] public string Name;
     ```

3. **Binary Formatter**:
   - You can use a binary formatter with `DataContractSerializer` for more compact serialization:
     ```csharp
     using (XmlDictionaryWriter w = XmlDictionaryWriter.CreateBinaryWriter(s))
     ```

4. **Handling Subclasses**:
   - The `NetDataContractSerializer` automatically handles subclasses, while `DataContractSerializer` requires you to specify known types using the `KnownType` attribute or during construction:
     ```csharp
     [DataContract, KnownType(typeof(Student)), KnownType(typeof(Teacher))]
     public class Person { ... }
     ```

5. **Object References**:
   - The `DataContractSerializer` does not preserve object references by default, meaning identical objects are serialized multiple times. You can enable this feature by setting `preserveObjectReferences` to true during construction:
     ```csharp
     var ds = new DataContractSerializer(typeof(Person), null, 1000, false, true, null);
     ```

6. **Serialization Performance**:
   - The `NetDataContractSerializer` may have performance issues with subtypes, while the `DataContractSerializer` requires knowledge of all subtypes for serialization.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using `DataContractSerializer` and `NetDataContractSerializer` for various object types and structures.
- Familiarize yourself with the use of attributes to control serialization behavior.
- Understand how to manage subclasses and object references during serialization.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into serialization in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on serialization concepts and the use of `DataContractSerializer` in C#.
