**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Serialization with DataContractSerializer**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **DataContractSerializer Basics**:
   - Serializes objects to XML, allowing customization of data contract names and namespaces using attributes.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataContract(Name="Candidate", Namespace="http://oreilly.com/nutshell")]
     public class Person { ... }
     ```

2. **DataMember Attributes**:
   - Control serialization of fields/properties, allowing for customization of member names and optional serialization.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataMember(Name="FirstName")] public string Name;
     ```

3. **Version Tolerance**:
   - Supports adding/removing data members without breaking compatibility. Unrecognized data can be stored using `IExtensibleDataObject`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataContract] public class Person : IExtensibleDataObject { ... }
     ```

4. **Required Members**:
   - Use `IsRequired` to enforce presence of essential members during deserialization.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataMember(IsRequired=true)] public int ID;
     ```

5. **Member Ordering**:
   - Serialization order is strict: base class members first, followed by ordered members, then alphabetical order.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataMember(Order=0)] public string Name;
     [DataMember(Order=1)] public int Age;
     ```

6. **Null and Empty Values**:
   - Control serialization of null/empty values using `EmitDefaultValue`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataMember(EmitDefaultValue=false)] public string Name;
     ```

7. **Data Contracts and Collections**:
   - Supports serialization of enumerable collections without type information, allowing flexibility in collection types.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataMember] public List<Address> Addresses;
     ```

8. **Subclassed Collection Elements**:
   - Handles subclassed elements transparently; requires declaration of valid subtypes.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataContract, KnownType(typeof(USAddress))]
     public class Address { ... }
     ```

9. **Customizing Collection and Element Names**:
   - Use `CollectionDataContract` to customize XML names for collections and dictionaries.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [CollectionDataContract(ItemName="Entry", KeyName="Kind", ValueName="Number")]
     public class PhoneNumberList : Dictionary<string, string> { ... }
     ```

10. **Serialization and Deserialization Hooks**:
    - Use attributes like `[OnSerializing]` and `[OnDeserialized]` to execute custom methods during serialization/deserialization.
    - Example:
      ```csharp
      [OnSerializing] void OnSerializingMethod(StreamingContext context) { ... }
      ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using `DataContractSerializer` for various object types and structures.
- Familiarize yourself with attributes to control serialization behavior and manage version tolerance.
- Understand how to handle collections and subclassed elements during serialization.

**Resources**:
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into serialization in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on serialization concepts and the use of `DataContractSerializer` in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Serialization Techniques**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **OnSerializing and OnDeserialized Attributes:**
   - These attributes are used to manage serialization for complex types that do not conform to standard serialization interfaces. 
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataContract]
     public class Person {
         public SerializationUnfriendlyType Addresses;
         [DataMember(Name="Addresses")]
         SerializationFriendlyType _serializationFriendlyAddresses;

         [OnSerializing]
         void PrepareForSerialization(StreamingContext sc) {
             // Copy Addresses to _serializationFriendlyAddresses
         }

         [OnDeserialized]
         void CompleteDeserialization(StreamingContext sc) {
             // Copy _serializationFriendlyAddresses back to Addresses
         }
     }
     ```

2. **Conditional Serialization:**
   - Use `OnSerializing` to conditionally serialize fields based on certain conditions.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public DateTime DateOfBirth;
     [DataMember] public bool Confidential;
     [DataMember(Name="DateOfBirth", EmitDefaultValue=false)]
     DateTime? _tempDateOfBirth;

     [OnSerializing]
     void PrepareForSerialization(StreamingContext sc) {
         _tempDateOfBirth = Confidential ? DateOfBirth : (DateTime?)null;
     }
     ```

3. **Initialization During Deserialization:**
   - The `OnDeserializing` method acts as a pseudoconstructor, initializing fields that are not serialized.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [DataContract]
     public class Test {
         bool _editable = true;

         [OnDeserializing]
         void Init(StreamingContext sc) {
             _editable = true;
         }
     }
     ```

4. **Interoperability with Binary Serialization:**
   - The `DataContractSerializer` can serialize types marked with binary serialization attributes, allowing for compatibility with legacy systems.
   - Types can be marked as serializable using `[Serializable]` or by implementing `ISerializable`.

5. **Binary Serialization:**
   - The binary serialization engine is efficient for complex object graphs but is not available in Windows Store apps.
   - Example of a serializable class:
     ```csharp
     [Serializable]
     public sealed class Person {
         public string Name;
         public int Age;
     }
     ```

6. **Using BinaryFormatter:**
   - The `BinaryFormatter` is used for serializing objects to a binary format.
   - Example of serialization:
     ```csharp
     Person p = new Person() { Name = "George", Age = 25 };
     IFormatter formatter = new BinaryFormatter();
     using (FileStream s = File.Create("serialized.bin")) {
         formatter.Serialize(s, p);
     }
     ```

7. **Deserialization:**
   - The deserializer reconstructs objects without calling constructors, restoring object references.
   - Example of deserialization:
     ```csharp
     using (FileStream s = File.OpenRead("serialized.bin")) {
         Person p2 = (Person)formatter.Deserialize(s);
         Console.WriteLine(p2.Name + " " + p2.Age); // Outputs: George 25
     }
     ```

8. **Performance Considerations:**
   - The performance of binary serialization can degrade with the number of references in the object graph, which is important for high-load scenarios.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using `OnSerializing` and `OnDeserialized` for managing complex serialization scenarios.
- Familiarize yourself with binary serialization and the use of `BinaryFormatter`.
- Understand the implications of interoperability between different serialization engines.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into serialization techniques in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on serialization techniques in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Binary Serialization Attributes**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **[NonSerialized] Attribute:**
   - Use this attribute to exclude fields from serialization. For example, if a field is used for temporary calculations, it should be marked as `[NonSerialized]`:
     ```csharp
     [Serializable]
     public sealed class Person {
         public string Name;
         public DateTime DateOfBirth;
         [NonSerialized] public int Age; // Not serialized
     }
     ```
   - Nonserialized members will always be null or default when deserialized.

2. **[OnDeserializing] and [OnDeserialized] Attributes:**
   - These attributes allow you to define methods that run before or after deserialization, respectively. This is useful for initializing fields that are not serialized:
     ```csharp
     [NonSerialized] public bool Valid = true;

     [OnDeserializing]
     void OnDeserializing(StreamingContext context) {
         Valid = true; // Set Valid to true during deserialization
     }

     [OnDeserialized]
     void OnDeserialized(StreamingContext context) {
         TimeSpan ts = DateTime.Now - DateOfBirth;
         Age = ts.Days / 365; // Calculate Age after deserialization
     }
     ```

3. **[OnSerializing] and [OnSerialized] Attributes:**
   - These attributes allow you to define methods that run before or after serialization. This can be useful for converting data types:
     ```csharp
     [Serializable]
     public sealed class Team {
         public string Name;
         [NonSerialized] public List<Person> Players = new List<Person>();
         Person[] _playersToSerialize;

         [OnSerializing]
         void OnSerializing(StreamingContext context) {
             _playersToSerialize = Players.ToArray(); // Convert List to array
         }

         [OnDeserialized]
         void OnDeserialized(StreamingContext context) {
             Players = new List<Person>(_playersToSerialize); // Convert back to List
         }
     }
     ```

4. **[OptionalField] Attribute:**
   - This attribute allows for backward compatibility when adding new fields. If a field is missing during deserialization, it will be treated as nonserialized:
     ```csharp
     [Serializable]
     public sealed class Person {
         public string Name;
         [OptionalField(VersionAdded = 2)] public DateTime DateOfBirth; // Optional field
     }
     ```

5. **Versioning Robustness:**
   - To maintain compatibility, avoid renaming or deleting fields and be cautious when adding `[NonSerialized]` attributes. Use versioning to manage changes effectively.

6. **Binary Serialization with ISerializable:**
   - Implementing the `ISerializable` interface gives complete control over serialization and deserialization:
     ```csharp
     public interface ISerializable {
         void GetObjectData(SerializationInfo info, StreamingContext context);
     }

     public class Team : ISerializable {
         public string Name;
         public List<Person> Players;

         public virtual void GetObjectData(SerializationInfo si, StreamingContext sc) {
             si.AddValue("Name", Name);
             si.AddValue("PlayerData", Players.ToArray()); // Serialize as array
         }

         protected Team(SerializationInfo si, StreamingContext sc) {
             Name = si.GetString("Name");
             Person[] a = (Person[])si.GetValue("PlayerData", typeof(Person[]));
             Players = new List<Person>(a); // Deserialize back to List
         }
     }
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using serialization attributes to manage the serialization process effectively.
- Familiarize yourself with implementing `ISerializable` for custom serialization control.
- Understand the implications of versioning and how to maintain compatibility across different versions of your classes.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into binary serialization in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on binary serialization concepts and techniques in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Serialization and Subclassing**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Subclassing Serializable Classes:**
   - When subclassing a serializable class, ensure that both the base and derived classes implement `ISerializable` if the base class is modified to control serialization. This prevents issues where derived class fields are not serialized.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [Serializable]
     public class Person : ISerializable {
         public string Name;
         public int Age;
         public virtual void GetObjectData(SerializationInfo si, StreamingContext sc) {
             si.AddValue("Name", Name);
             si.AddValue("Age", Age);
         }
         protected Person(SerializationInfo si, StreamingContext sc) {
             Name = si.GetString("Name");
             Age = si.GetInt32("Age");
         }
         public Person() {}
     }

     [Serializable]
     public class Student : Person {
         public string Course;
         public override void GetObjectData(SerializationInfo si, StreamingContext sc) {
             base.GetObjectData(si, sc);
             si.AddValue("Course", Course);
         }
         protected Student(SerializationInfo si, StreamingContext sc) : base(si, sc) {
             Course = si.GetString("Course");
         }
         public Student() {}
     }
     ```

2. **XML Serialization:**
   - The `XmlSerializer` in the `System.Xml.Serialization` namespace is used for serializing .NET types to XML. It can serialize public fields and properties by default.
   - Example of serialization:
     ```csharp
     public class Person {
         public string Name;
         public int Age;
     }

     Person p = new Person { Name = "Stacey", Age = 30 };
     XmlSerializer xs = new XmlSerializer(typeof(Person));
     using (Stream s = File.Create("person.xml")) {
         xs.Serialize(s, p);
     }
     ```

3. **Attributes for XML Serialization:**
   - Use attributes like `[XmlIgnore]`, `[XmlElement]`, and `[XmlAttribute]` to control serialization behavior and customize XML output.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Person {
         [XmlIgnore] public DateTime DateOfBirth;
         [XmlElement("FirstName")] public string Name;
         [XmlAttribute("RoughAge")] public int Age;
     }
     ```

4. **Handling Subclasses:**
   - To serialize subclasses, use the `[XmlInclude]` attribute or specify subclasses when constructing the `XmlSerializer`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [XmlInclude(typeof(Student))]
     [XmlInclude(typeof(Teacher))]
     public class Person { public string Name; }
     ```

5. **Child Objects Serialization:**
   - `XmlSerializer` automatically serializes child objects, but if an object is referenced multiple times, it will be serialized multiple times unless a different serialization engine is used.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Person {
         public string Name;
         public Address HomeAddress = new Address();
     }
     ```

6. **Subclassing Child Objects:**
   - When serializing a class that can reference subclasses, ensure the XML structure matches the desired output, using type attributes to indicate the specific subclass.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Address { public string Street, PostCode; }
     public class USAddress : Address { }
     public class AUAddress : Address { }
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice implementing `ISerializable` for classes that may be subclassed.
- Familiarize yourself with XML serialization attributes and their effects on the serialized output.
- Understand how to manage serialization of subclasses and child objects effectively.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into serialization and subclassing in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on serialization concepts and subclassing in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: XML Serialization and Subclassing**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Subclassing with XML Serialization:**
   - Use `[XmlInclude]` to register subclasses of a base class for XML serialization. This allows the serializer to recognize and handle derived types.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [XmlInclude(typeof(AUAddress))]
     [XmlInclude(typeof(USAddress))]
     public class Address {
         public string Street, PostCode;
     }
     ```

2. **Element Naming:**
   - To reflect the subtype name in the XML output, stack multiple `[XmlElement]` attributes on the property in the parent class.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Person {
         public string Name;
         [XmlElement("Address", typeof(Address))]
         [XmlElement("AUAddress", typeof(AUAddress))]
         [XmlElement("USAddress", typeof(USAddress))]
         public Address HomeAddress = new USAddress();
     }
     ```

3. **Serializing Collections:**
   - The `XmlSerializer` can automatically serialize concrete collection types, such as `List<T>`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Person {
         public string Name;
         public List<Address> Addresses = new List<Address>();
     }
     ```

4. **Customizing Collection Serialization:**
   - Use `[XmlArray]` to rename the outer element and `[XmlArrayItem]` to rename inner elements.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Person {
         public string Name;
         [XmlArray("PreviousAddresses")]
         [XmlArrayItem("Location")]
         public List<Address> Addresses = new List<Address>();
     }
     ```

5. **Subclassed Collection Elements:**
   - To encode subclassed elements with type attributes, add `[XmlInclude]` to the base class.
   - For naming subclassed elements according to their type, stack multiple `[XmlArrayItem]` or `[XmlElement]` attributes on the collection property.

6. **IXmlSerializable Interface:**
   - For more control over XML serialization, implement the `IXmlSerializable` interface, which allows custom read and write methods.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     public class Address : IXmlSerializable {
         public string Street, PostCode;
         public void ReadXml(XmlReader reader) { /* Custom read logic */ }
         public void WriteXml(XmlWriter writer) { /* Custom write logic */ }
     }
     ```

**Preparation Tips:**
- Practice using XML serialization attributes to manage serialization of classes and collections effectively.
- Familiarize yourself with the `IXmlSerializable` interface for custom serialization scenarios.
- Understand how to handle subclassing and collections in XML serialization.

**Resources:**
- Use LINQPad and Visual Studio for hands-on coding practice.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into XML serialization in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on XML serialization and subclassing in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Assemblies and Manifest**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Assembly Manifest**:
   - Contains essential data about the assembly, including:
     - Simple name, version number, public key, and referenced assemblies.
     - List of modules and types defined within the assembly.
     - Security permissions and culture information.
   - Informational data includes title, description, company, copyright, and display version.

2. **Viewing Assembly Manifest**:
   - Use `ildasm.exe` to view the contents of an assembly's manifest.

3. **Specifying Assembly Attributes**:
   - Control manifest content using assembly attributes in `AssemblyInfo.cs`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [assembly: AssemblyCopyright("© Corp Ltd. All rights reserved.")]
     [assembly: AssemblyVersion("2.3.2.1")]
     ```

4. **Application Manifest**:
   - An XML file that provides information about the assembly to the OS, influencing application launch.
   - Example of requesting administrative elevation:
     ```xml
     <assembly manifestVersion="1.0" xmlns="urn:schemas-microsoft-com:asm.v1">
       <trustInfo xmlns="urn:schemas-microsoft-com:asm.v2">
         <security>
           <requestedPrivileges>
             <requestedExecutionLevel level="requireAdministrator" />
           </requestedPrivileges>
         </security>
       </trustInfo>
     </assembly>
     ```

5. **Deploying Application Manifest**:
   - Can be deployed as a separate file or embedded within the assembly.
   - Separate file must match the assembly name with a `.manifest` extension.

6. **Modules**:
   - Assemblies can consist of multiple modules, allowing for code compiled in different languages.
   - The main module contains the manifest, while additional modules may contain IL or resources.

7. **Assembly Class**:
   - Access assembly metadata at runtime using the `Assembly` class in `System.Reflection`.
   - Obtain an assembly object via a type's `Assembly` property or static methods like `GetExecutingAssembly`.

8. **Strong Names and Assembly Signing**:
   - Strongly named assemblies have a unique identity and are tamper-proof, using a public/private key pair.
   - Strong-name signing ensures that the assembly cannot be modified without breaking the signature.

9. **How to Strongly Name an Assembly**:
   - Generate a key pair using `sn.exe` and compile with the `/keyfile` switch.
   - Strongly named assemblies cannot reference weakly named assemblies.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Understand the structure and purpose of assembly manifests and how to manipulate them.
- Familiarize yourself with assembly attributes and their impact on assembly metadata.
- Practice using the `Assembly` class for reflection and metadata access.
- Learn the importance of strong naming and how to implement it in your projects.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and command-line tools for hands-on practice with assemblies and manifests.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into assemblies and their management in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on assemblies and manifest concepts in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Assemblies and Signing**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Key Pair Usage**:
   - A single key pair can sign multiple assemblies with distinct identities based on their simple names. The choice of key pair usage depends on organizational needs, balancing security and management.

2. **Delay Signing**:
   - Delay signing allows developers to work with assemblies without full access to private keys. Assemblies are flagged with a public key but not fully signed, enabling testing while maintaining security. Final signing occurs before deployment.

3. **Assembly Identity**:
   - An assembly's identity consists of its simple name, version, culture, and public key token. The simple name is derived from the file name, while the version follows the format major.minor.build.revision.

4. **Fully Qualified Names**:
   - A fully qualified assembly name includes all identity components in a specific format. For example: `"System.Xml, Version=2.0.0.0, Culture=neutral, PublicKeyToken=b77a5c561934e089"`.

5. **AssemblyName Class**:
   - The `AssemblyName` class helps parse or build fully qualified assembly names and contains properties for each identity component. It can be instantiated directly or obtained from an existing assembly.

6. **Assembly Informational and File Versions**:
   - `AssemblyInformationalVersion` and `AssemblyFileVersion` provide additional versioning information that does not affect assembly identity but is useful for user display and build tracking.

7. **Authenticode Signing**:
   - Authenticode is a code-signing system that verifies the publisher's identity, providing assurance that the assembly has not been modified. It is separate from strong-name signing and is essential for applications distributed online.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Understand the implications of key pair management and delay signing in assembly security.
- Familiarize yourself with assembly identity components and how they affect versioning and compatibility.
- Practice using the `AssemblyName` class for manipulating assembly names.
- Learn about Authenticode signing and its importance in application distribution.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and command-line tools for hands-on practice with assemblies and signing.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into assembly management and security in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on assemblies, signing, and versioning in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Authenticode and Assemblies**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Authenticode Overview**:
   - Authenticode is used for signing .NET assemblies, unmanaged executables, ActiveX controls, and .msi files to verify the publisher's identity.
   - It does not guarantee malware-free software but indicates that a trusted entity backs the executable.

2. **Signing Process**:
   - To sign with Authenticode, obtain an X.509 code-signing certificate from a Certificate Authority (CA) after verifying your identity.
   - Use the `signtool` utility to sign assemblies, specifying the certificate's thumbprint or file.

3. **Certificate Management**:
   - Certificates can be stored in a password-protected file or the computer's certificate store for easier access during signing.
   - Strong-name signing should occur before Authenticode signing to avoid tampering issues.

4. **Time Stamping**:
   - Use a timestamping server when signing to ensure that the signature remains valid even after the certificate expires.

5. **Authenticode Validation**:
   - Windows and the CLR validate Authenticode signatures. The CLR can check the signature when requested, avoiding performance issues seen in earlier versions.

6. **Global Assembly Cache (GAC)**:
   - The GAC is a central repository for .NET assemblies, allowing for machine-level versioning.
   - It simplifies version management for shared assemblies but complicates deployment and updates, requiring administrative privileges.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Understand the process of obtaining and using Authenticode certificates.
- Familiarize yourself with the `signtool` utility for signing assemblies.
- Learn about the implications of using the GAC for assembly management and versioning.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and command-line tools for hands-on practice with Authenticode signing and assembly management.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into Authenticode and assemblies in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on Authenticode signing and assembly management in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Global Assembly Cache (GAC) and Resources**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Global Assembly Cache (GAC):**
   - The GAC improves startup time for large assemblies by verifying signatures only once upon installation.
   - Assemblies in the GAC are fully trusted, even when called from limited-permission environments.

2. **Installing Assemblies to the GAC:**
   - Assemblies must have a strong name before installation.
   - Use the command `gacutil /i MyAssembly.dll` to install, and `gacutil /u MyAssembly` to uninstall.
   - The GAC allows applications to reference assemblies without needing local copies, but local copies are ignored if a GAC version exists.

3. **Versioning in the GAC:**
   - Changing an assembly's version creates a new identity, allowing side-by-side execution of multiple versions.
   - Applications referencing older versions continue to function, preventing "DLL hell."
   - Options for updates include reinstalling the same version or using a new version, with the latter requiring recompilation of dependent applications.

4. **Resources and Satellite Assemblies:**
   - Resources can include images, text, or XML files, represented as byte streams in assemblies.
   - Localized resources can be packaged as satellite assemblies, which are automatically selected based on the user's language.

5. **Embedding Resources:**
   - Use the `/resource` switch during compilation to embed resources directly into assemblies.
   - In Visual Studio, set the build action of files to "Embedded Resource" to include them in the assembly.

6. **Accessing Resources:**
   - Use `GetManifestResourceStream` to retrieve embedded resources from an assembly.
   - Resource names are case-sensitive and typically prefixed with the project's default namespace.

7. **.resources and .resx Files:**
   - .resources files are binary containers for localizable content, while .resx files are XML-based design-time formats for creating .resources files.
   - Visual Studio automates the conversion of .resx files to .resources during compilation.

**Preparation Tips:**
- Understand the GAC's role in assembly management and how to install/uninstall assemblies.
- Familiarize yourself with versioning strategies and the implications of side-by-side execution.
- Practice embedding and accessing resources in assemblies, including localization techniques.

**Resources:**
- Use Visual Studio and command-line tools for hands-on practice with GAC and resource management.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into assemblies and resources in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on the Global Assembly Cache, versioning, and resource management in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Resources and Satellite Assemblies**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Resource Management**:
   - Use `.resx` files for managing resources in applications. The `ResXResourceWriter` class can create these files programmatically.
   - Images should be treated as binary data in `.resx` files, allowing for more versatility than typed Image objects.

2. **Creating and Converting .resx Files**:
   - Create a `.resx` file using `ResXResourceWriter` and convert it to a `.resources` file using the `resgen` command.
   - Example of adding binary data:
     ```xml
     <data name="flag.png" type="System.Byte[], mscorlib">
     <value>Qk32BAAAAAAAAHYAAAAoAAAAMAMDAwACAgIAAAAD/AA....</value>
     </data>
     ```

3. **Reading .resources Files**:
   - Use the `ResourceManager` class to read resources from `.resources` files embedded in assemblies.
   - Access resources using methods like `GetString` and `GetObject`.

4. **WPF Resource Management**:
   - In WPF applications, resources must be added to the project and set to "Resource" build action for URI access.
   - Use `Application.GetResourceStream` to load resources programmatically.

5. **Satellite Assemblies**:
   - Satellite assemblies contain localized resources for different languages, allowing applications to adapt to the user's language settings.
   - The main assembly holds default resources, while satellite assemblies are named using language codes (e.g., `de` for German).

6. **Building and Testing Satellite Assemblies**:
   - Create localized `.resx` files (e.g., `welcome.de.resx`) for different languages and build satellite assemblies using the `al` command.
   - Test satellite assemblies by changing the `CurrentUICulture` of the thread.

7. **Visual Studio Designer Support**:
   - Visual Studio provides tools for localizing components, allowing properties to be persisted in `.resx` files based on the selected language.

8. **Culture and Subculture Management**:
   - Cultures represent languages, while subcultures represent regional variations. Use `CultureInfo` to manage and examine current cultures in applications.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice creating and managing `.resx` and `.resources` files.
- Familiarize yourself with the `ResourceManager` class for accessing resources.
- Understand how to implement and test satellite assemblies for localization.
- Explore Visual Studio's localization features for UI components.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and command-line tools for hands-on practice with resource management and satellite assemblies.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into localization and resource handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on resource management and localization in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Assembly Resolution and Resource Management**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **ResourceManager and Culture**:
   - The `ResourceManager` uses the current thread's `CurrentUICulture` to load the appropriate satellite assembly. It employs a fallback mechanism, first checking for subculture assemblies, then generic culture, and finally the default culture in the main assembly.

2. **Assembly Resolution**:
   - Assembly resolution involves locating referenced assemblies during both compile time and runtime. At compile time, paths are provided, while at runtime, the CLR checks the GAC, probing paths, and may trigger the `AssemblyResolve` event if the assembly isn't found.

3. **Assembly and Type Resolution**:
   - Types are scoped to assemblies, which serve as unique identifiers. The CLR loads assemblies when their types are first referenced, checking if the assembly is already loaded in memory.

4. **AssemblyResolve Event**:
   - This event allows custom handling of assembly loading when the CLR cannot find an assembly. You can define a method to locate and load the assembly manually.

5. **Loading Assemblies**:
   - Use `Assembly.Load`, `LoadFrom`, or `LoadFile` to load assemblies. `Load` uses the CLR's resolution system, while `LoadFrom` and `LoadFile` load from specific file paths, with `LoadFrom` hinting at onward references.

6. **Differences Between LoadFrom and LoadFile**:
   - `LoadFrom` returns the existing assembly if already loaded, while `LoadFile` provides a new instance. `LoadFrom` also helps resolve dependencies, whereas `LoadFile` does not.

7. **Static vs. Dynamic References**:
   - Statically referenced types are compiled into the assembly, while dynamically loaded types can be instantiated using reflection. Mixing these can lead to multiple copies of the same assembly in memory.

8. **Avoiding Duplicate Assemblies**:
   - To prevent loading multiple versions of the same assembly, check if it exists in the application base directory before using `LoadFrom` or `LoadFile`.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Understand the assembly resolution process and how to manage resources effectively.
- Familiarize yourself with the `ResourceManager` and its fallback mechanisms.
- Practice handling the `AssemblyResolve` event for custom assembly loading.
- Learn the differences between loading methods and their implications on assembly management.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and command-line tools for hands-on practice with assembly loading and resource management.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into assembly resolution and resource handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on assembly resolution and resource management in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Assembly Loading and Management**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **AssemblyResolve Event**:
   - The `AssemblyResolve` event is triggered when the CLR cannot find an assembly. This allows for custom loading of assemblies from specified paths, bypassing the default probing paths.

2. **Loading Assemblies**:
   - Use `LoadFrom`, `LoadFile`, or load from a byte array to load assemblies. The CLR first checks the Global Assembly Cache (GAC) before looking in other locations.
   - Loading from a byte array requires handling the `AssemblyResolve` event to resolve dependencies.

3. **Location vs. CodeBase**:
   - The `Location` property returns the physical file path of the assembly, while `CodeBase` provides the URI. Special cases include shadow copied assemblies, where `Location` may be blank.

4. **Deploying Assemblies**:
   - Assemblies can be deployed outside the application base directory. To assist the CLR in finding these assemblies, handle the `AssemblyResolve` event to specify custom paths.

5. **Packing Single-File Executables**:
   - To create a single executable from multiple assemblies, embed DLLs as resources and use the `AssemblyResolve` event to load them on demand. This avoids the need for separate files during deployment.

6. **Working with Unreferenced Assemblies**:
   - Use `ExecuteAssembly` to run executables directly. For libraries, use `LoadFrom` and reflection to access types and methods dynamically.

7. **Reflection and Dynamic Loading**:
   - Reflection allows for loading types from assemblies that may not have been referenced at compile time. This is useful for plugin architectures where types are instantiated at runtime.

8. **Interface-Based Communication**:
   - Define common interfaces in shared assemblies to facilitate communication between dynamically loaded types, ensuring compatibility across different versions of assemblies.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice handling the `AssemblyResolve` event for custom assembly loading.
- Familiarize yourself with the differences between `LoadFrom`, `LoadFile`, and loading from byte arrays.
- Understand how to embed resources in executables and manage assembly dependencies.
- Explore reflection for dynamic type loading and method invocation.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and command-line tools for hands-on practice with assembly loading and management.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into assembly resolution and dynamic loading in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on assembly loading, management, and dynamic type handling in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Assembly Loading and Reflection**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Dynamic Assembly Loading**:
   - Use `Assembly.LoadFrom` to load assemblies dynamically from a specified directory. This allows for runtime flexibility but can introduce risks if rogue assemblies are added.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     string dir = AppDomain.CurrentDomain.BaseDirectory;
     foreach (string assFile in Directory.GetFiles(dir, "*Server.dll"))
     {
         Assembly a = Assembly.LoadFrom(assFile);
         foreach (Type t in a.GetTypes())
         {
             if (typeof(MyBaseServerType).IsAssignableFrom(t))
             {
                 // Expose type t
             }
         }
     }
     ```

2. **Public Key Verification**:
   - To ensure that only trusted assemblies are loaded, compare the public key of the assembly being loaded with the public key of the executing assembly.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     byte[] ourPK = Assembly.GetExecutingAssembly().GetName().GetPublicKey();
     foreach (string assFile in Directory.GetFiles(dir, "*Server.dll"))
     {
         byte[] targetPK = AssemblyName.GetAssemblyName(assFile).GetPublicKey();
         if (Enumerable.SequenceEqual(ourPK, targetPK))
         {
             Assembly a = Assembly.LoadFrom(assFile);
             // Proceed with loading
         }
     }
     ```

3. **Reflection Basics**:
   - Reflection allows inspection of metadata, types, and members at runtime. It is essential for dynamic binding, serialization, and other advanced features.
   - Use `System.Reflection` to access type information and metadata.

4. **Obtaining Type Information**:
   - Use `GetType()` or `typeof` to obtain a `System.Type` instance, which provides metadata about the type.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type t1 = DateTime.Now.GetType();
     Type t2 = typeof(DateTime);
     ```

5. **Working with Arrays and Generics**:
   - Use `MakeArrayType` to create array types dynamically and `GetElementType` to retrieve the element type of an array.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type simpleArrayType = typeof(int).MakeArrayType();
     Type cubeType = typeof(int).MakeArrayType(3);
     ```

6. **Nested Types**:
   - Retrieve nested types using `GetNestedTypes()`. Nested types have special accessibility levels.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     foreach (Type t in typeof(System.Environment).GetNestedTypes())
     {
         Console.WriteLine(t.FullName);
     }
     ```

7. **Type Properties**:
   - Access properties like `Namespace`, `Name`, `FullName`, and `AssemblyQualifiedName` to get detailed information about a type.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type t = typeof(System.Text.StringBuilder);
     Console.WriteLine(t.Namespace); // System.Text
     Console.WriteLine(t.Name);      // StringBuilder
     Console.WriteLine(t.FullName);   // System.Text.StringBuilder
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice loading assemblies dynamically and verifying their public keys to ensure security.
- Familiarize yourself with reflection to inspect types and metadata at runtime.
- Understand how to work with arrays, generics, and nested types using reflection.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with assembly loading and reflection.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into assembly management and reflection in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on assembly loading, reflection, and type management in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Reflection and Metadata**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Generic Type Names**:
   - Generic types are suffixed with an apostrophe followed by the number of type parameters. Closed generic types include the full assembly-qualified names of their type parameters in their `FullName`.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Console.WriteLine(typeof(Dictionary<int, string>).FullName);
     // Outputs: System.Collections.Generic.Dictionary'2[[System.Int32, mscorlib, ...], [System.String, mscorlib, ...]]
     ```

2. **Array and Pointer Types**:
   - Array types are represented with the same suffix as in a `typeof` expression.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Console.WriteLine(typeof(int[]).Name); // Outputs: Int32[]
     Console.WriteLine(typeof(byte*).Name); // Outputs: Byte*
     ```

3. **Ref and Out Parameter Types**:
   - Types for `ref` or `out` parameters have an `&` suffix.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type t = typeof(bool).GetMethod("TryParse").GetParameters()[1].ParameterType;
     Console.WriteLine(t.Name); // Outputs: Boolean&
     ```

4. **Base Types and Interfaces**:
   - Use the `BaseType` property to get the base class of a type.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type baseType = typeof(System.String).BaseType;
     Console.WriteLine(baseType.Name); // Outputs: Object
     ```

5. **Type Checking**:
   - Use `IsInstanceOfType` and `IsAssignableFrom` for dynamic type checking.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     object obj = Guid.NewGuid();
     bool isTrue = obj is IFormattable; // Static check
     bool alsoTrue = typeof(IFormattable).IsInstanceOfType(obj); // Dynamic check
     ```

6. **Instantiating Types**:
   - Use `Activator.CreateInstance` to dynamically create instances of types.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     List<int> list = (List<int>)Activator.CreateInstance(typeof(List<int>));
     ```

7. **Dynamic Instantiation of Arrays and Delegates**:
   - Use `MakeArrayType` for arrays and `Delegate.CreateDelegate` for delegates.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Delegate staticD = Delegate.CreateDelegate(typeof(IntFunc), typeof(Program), "Square");
     ```

8. **Generic Types**:
   - Closed generic types can be instantiated, while unbound types cannot. Use `MakeGenericType` to create closed types from unbound types.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type unbound = typeof(List<>);
     Type closed = unbound.MakeGenericType(typeof(int));
     ```

9. **Reflection and Member Access**:
   - Use `GetMembers` to retrieve members of a type.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     MemberInfo[] members = typeof(Walnut).GetMembers();
     foreach (MemberInfo m in members) Console.WriteLine(m);
     ```

10. **TypeInfo for Reflection**:
    - `TypeInfo` provides a simpler protocol for reflecting over members, returning `IEnumerable<T>` for LINQ queries.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using reflection to inspect types, members, and instantiate objects dynamically.
- Familiarize yourself with generic types and their instantiation.
- Understand how to check type compatibility and access base types and interfaces.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with reflection and metadata.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into reflection in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on reflection and metadata in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Reflection and Member Invocation**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Declared Members**:
   - Use `GetTypeInfo().DeclaredMembers` to retrieve only the members declared in a type, excluding inherited members. This includes methods, properties, and fields.

2. **Specific Member Retrieval**:
   - Methods like `GetDeclaredMethod` can retrieve specific members by name, but for overloaded methods, use LINQ to filter by name and parameter count.

3. **MemberInfo Class**:
   - The `MemberInfo` class provides metadata about members, including properties like `Name`, `DeclaringType`, and `ReflectedType`. The `MemberType` property indicates the type of member (e.g., Method, Property).

4. **Filtering Members**:
   - Use `GetMembers` with a `MemberTypes` filter to restrict results to specific member types. Alternatively, use methods like `GetMethods`, `GetProperties`, etc., for more targeted retrieval.

5. **MethodInfo and MemberInfo Comparison**:
   - `MethodInfo` is a subclass of `MemberInfo`. You can compare methods from different types using `MethodHandle` or `MetadataToken` to check if they refer to the same method.

6. **Dynamic Invocation**:
   - Once you have a `MethodInfo`, `PropertyInfo`, or `FieldInfo`, you can invoke or access them dynamically. This is known as dynamic binding.

7. **Backing Methods**:
   - Properties and events in C# have backing methods (e.g., `get_XXX`, `set_XXX`). Use `GetGetMethod` and `GetSetMethod` to access these methods.

8. **Generic Types**:
   - Member metadata can be obtained for both unbound and closed generic types. Unbound types cannot be invoked dynamically.

9. **LINQ for Member Queries**:
   - Use LINQ to query members, such as finding a property associated with a specific method.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using reflection to inspect and invoke members dynamically.
- Familiarize yourself with the `MemberInfo` class and its subclasses for retrieving member metadata.
- Understand how to filter and retrieve specific members to avoid issues with overloads.
- Explore the differences between unbound and closed generic types in reflection.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with reflection and member invocation.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into reflection and metadata in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on reflection, member retrieval, and dynamic invocation in C#.

---

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Reflection and Dynamic Invocation**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Dynamic Property Access**:
   - Use reflection to access properties dynamically. For example, to get the length of a string:
     ```csharp
     object s = "Hello";
     PropertyInfo prop = s.GetType().GetProperty("Length");
     int length = (int)prop.GetValue(s, null); // length = 5
     ```

2. **Dynamic Method Invocation**:
   - Call methods dynamically using `MethodInfo.Invoke`. For instance, to call the `Substring` method:
     ```csharp
     Type type = typeof(string);
     MethodInfo method = type.GetMethod("Substring", new Type[] { typeof(int) });
     object returnValue = method.Invoke("stamp", new object[] { 2 }); // returnValue = "amp"
     ```

3. **Handling Method Parameters**:
   - Use `GetParameters` to retrieve parameter metadata for methods. For `ref` or `out` parameters, use `MakeByRefType`:
     ```csharp
     Type[] argTypes = { typeof(string), typeof(int).MakeByRefType() };
     MethodInfo tryParse = typeof(int).GetMethod("TryParse", argTypes);
     ```

4. **Generic Method Retrieval**:
   - To retrieve generic methods, filter methods by name and check for generic parameters. Use `MakeGenericMethod` to close generic types:
     ```csharp
     var closedMethod = unboundMethod.MakeGenericMethod(typeof(int));
     ```

5. **Using Delegates for Performance**:
   - For repeated dynamic method calls, create a delegate to improve performance:
     ```csharp
     delegate string StringToString(string s);
     MethodInfo trimMethod = typeof(string).GetMethod("Trim");
     var trim = (StringToString)Delegate.CreateDelegate(typeof(StringToString), trimMethod);
     ```

6. **Accessing Nonpublic Members**:
   - Use `BindingFlags` to access nonpublic members. For example, to access a private field:
     ```csharp
     FieldInfo f = typeof(Walnut).GetField("cracked", BindingFlags.NonPublic | BindingFlags.Instance);
     ```

7. **BindingFlags Enum**:
   - Combine `BindingFlags` to filter member retrieval. For example, to get all public static members:
     ```csharp
     MemberInfo[] members = typeof(object).GetMembers(BindingFlags.Public | BindingFlags.Static);
     ```

8. **Generic Method Invocation**:
   - Generic methods require `MakeGenericMethod` to specify type arguments before invocation:
     ```csharp
     MethodInfo echo = typeof(Program).GetMethod("Echo");
     MethodInfo intEcho = echo.MakeGenericMethod(typeof(int));
     ```

9. **Anonymous Invocation of Generic Interfaces**:
   - Use reflection to invoke members of generic interfaces when type parameters are unknown at compile time.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using reflection to dynamically access properties and methods.
- Familiarize yourself with handling method parameters, especially `ref` and `out`.
- Understand how to retrieve and invoke generic methods dynamically.
- Explore performance optimization techniques using delegates for repeated method calls.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with reflection and dynamic invocation.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into reflection and dynamic method handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on reflection, dynamic invocation, and member access in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Reflection and Member Invocation**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Dynamic Member Access**:
   - Use reflection to access members of generic types, such as `List<>` and `IGrouping<,>`, by leveraging non-generic interfaces like `IList` for collections.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     if (value is IList)
         sb.Append("A list with " + ((IList)value).Count + " items");
     ```

2. **Handling Generic Interfaces**:
   - For interfaces like `IGrouping<,>`, use reflection to access properties like `Key` by first determining the closed generic type.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type closedIGrouping = value.GetType().GetInterfaces()
         .FirstOrDefault(t => t.IsGenericType && t.GetGenericTypeDefinition() == typeof(IGrouping<,>));
     ```

3. **Reflection for Assembly Inspection**:
   - Retrieve types from an assembly using `GetType` or `GetTypes`. Use `Assembly.LoadFrom` to load assemblies from a file.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Assembly a = Assembly.LoadFrom(@"e:\demo\mylib.dll");
     foreach (Type t in a.GetTypes())
         Console.WriteLine(t);
     ```

4. **Reflection-Only Context**:
   - Load assemblies into a reflection-only context to inspect type information without executing static constructors.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Assembly a = Assembly.ReflectionOnlyLoadFrom(@"e:\demo\mylib.dll");
     ```

5. **Modules and Metadata Tokens**:
   - Understand that assemblies can contain multiple modules, and metadata tokens uniquely identify types and members within a module.
   - Use `GetModules` to list all modules in an assembly.

6. **Working with Attributes**:
   - Attributes provide additional metadata for types and members. Custom attributes can be defined and retrieved at runtime.
   - Example of defining a custom attribute:
     ```csharp
     [Obsolete] public class Foo {...}
     ```

7. **Types of Attributes**:
   - Attributes can be bit-mapped, custom, or pseudocustom. Custom attributes are extensible and can be created by users.

8. **Reflection API for Attributes**:
   - Use reflection to access attributes applied to types or members, utilizing methods like `GetCustomAttributes`.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice using reflection to dynamically access and invoke members, especially for generic types.
- Familiarize yourself with loading assemblies and inspecting their types in both normal and reflection-only contexts.
- Understand how to work with attributes and their implications in C#.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with reflection and attributes.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into reflection and metadata handling in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on reflection, member invocation, and attribute management in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Attributes and Reflection**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Pseudocustom Attributes**:
   - Pseudocustom attributes, like standard custom attributes, are subclasses of `System.Attribute` but are optimized by the CLR as bit-mapped attributes. Examples include `[Serializable]` and `[StructLayout]`.

2. **AttributeUsage Attribute**:
   - The `AttributeUsage` attribute specifies how an attribute can be used, including whether it can be applied multiple times, if it is inherited, and the valid targets (e.g., classes, methods). 
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     [AttributeUsage(AttributeTargets.Class | AttributeTargets.Struct)]
     public sealed class SerializableAttribute : Attribute { }
     ```

3. **Defining Custom Attributes**:
   - To create a custom attribute:
     1. Derive from `System.Attribute`.
     2. Apply `AttributeUsage`.
     3. Define public constructors for positional parameters.
     4. Declare public fields or properties for named parameters.
   - Example of a custom attribute for unit testing:
     ```csharp
     [AttributeUsage(AttributeTargets.Method)]
     public sealed class TestAttribute : Attribute {
         public int Repetitions;
         public string FailureMessage;
         public TestAttribute(int repetitions = 1) { Repetitions = repetitions; }
     }
     ```

4. **Retrieving Attributes at Runtime**:
   - Use `GetCustomAttributes` or `Attribute.GetCustomAttribute` to retrieve attributes from types or members.
   - Example of enumerating methods with a specific attribute:
     ```csharp
     foreach (MethodInfo mi in typeof(Foo).GetMethods()) {
         TestAttribute att = (TestAttribute)Attribute.GetCustomAttribute(mi, typeof(TestAttribute));
         if (att != null) {
             Console.WriteLine($"Method {mi.Name} will be tested; reps={att.Repetitions}; msg={att.FailureMessage}");
         }
     }
     ```

5. **Reflection-Only Context**:
   - In a reflection-only context, you cannot instantiate attributes. Use `CustomAttributeData` to reflect over attributes without instantiation.

6. **Dynamic Code Generation**:
   - The `System.Reflection.Emit` namespace allows for runtime generation of metadata and IL. This is useful for tasks like creating dynamic proxies or types for specific operations.
   - The `DynamicMethod` class enables the creation of methods on the fly without needing a full dynamic assembly setup.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice defining and using custom attributes, including handling positional and named parameters.
- Familiarize yourself with retrieving attributes using reflection and understanding the differences in contexts (normal vs. reflection-only).
- Explore dynamic code generation techniques using `Reflection.Emit` and `DynamicMethod`.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with attributes and reflection.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into attributes and dynamic code generation in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on attributes, reflection, and dynamic code generation in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Dynamic Methods and IL Generation**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **DynamicMethod Overview**:
   - Dynamic methods are created at runtime and can be garbage-collected when no longer referenced, allowing for efficient memory use.
   - Example of a simple dynamic method that prints "Hello world":
     ```csharp
     var dynMeth = new DynamicMethod("Foo", null, null, typeof(Test));
     ILGenerator gen = dynMeth.GetILGenerator();
     gen.EmitWriteLine("Hello world");
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ret);
     dynMeth.Invoke(null, null);
     ```

2. **ILGenerator and OpCodes**:
   - The `ILGenerator` class is used to emit Intermediate Language (IL) instructions. Each IL operation is represented by an `OpCode`.
   - Example of using `OpCodes` to call a method:
     ```csharp
     MethodInfo writeLineStr = typeof(Console).GetMethod("WriteLine", new Type[] { typeof(string) });
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldstr, "Hello world");
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Call, writeLineStr);
     ```

3. **Evaluation Stack**:
   - IL uses an evaluation stack for method calls. Arguments are pushed onto the stack before calling a method, which then pops them off.
   - Example of adding two integers:
     ```csharp
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldc_I4, 2); // Push 2
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldc_I4, 2); // Push 2
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Add);       // Add them
     ```

4. **Passing Arguments**:
   - Use `Ldarg` opcodes to load method arguments onto the stack. Ensure one value remains on the stack for return.
   - Example of a dynamic method that returns the sum of two integers:
     ```csharp
     DynamicMethod dynMeth = new DynamicMethod("Foo", typeof(int), new[] { typeof(int), typeof(int) }, typeof(void));
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldarg_0); // Load first argument
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldarg_1); // Load second argument
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Add);      // Add them
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ret);      // Return
     ```

5. **Local Variables**:
   - Declare local variables using `DeclareLocal` and manipulate them with `Ldloc` and `Stloc`.
   - Example of dynamically generating code that multiplies two integers:
     ```csharp
     LocalBuilder localX = gen.DeclareLocal(typeof(int));
     LocalBuilder localY = gen.DeclareLocal(typeof(int));
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldc_I4, 6);
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Stloc, localX);
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldc_I4, 7);
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Stloc, localY);
     ```

6. **Branching**:
   - IL uses labels and branching opcodes (e.g., `Br`, `Brtrue`, `Blt`) for control flow instead of traditional loops.
   - Example of emitting a while loop:
     ```csharp
     Label loopStart = gen.DefineLabel();
     gen.MarkLabel(loopStart);
     // Emit loop logic here
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice creating dynamic methods and using `ILGenerator` to emit IL instructions.
- Familiarize yourself with the evaluation stack and how to manipulate it with opcodes.
- Understand how to declare local variables and implement branching logic in IL.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and tools like LINQPad for hands-on practice with dynamic methods and IL generation.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into dynamic code generation and IL. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on dynamic methods, IL generation, and reflection in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Dynamic Code Generation and Reflection**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Dynamic Method Creation**:
   - Use `DynamicMethod` to create methods at runtime. The `ILGenerator` class is used to emit Intermediate Language (IL) instructions.
   - Example: Create a loop that increments a variable until it exceeds a certain value.

2. **IL Instructions**:
   - Use opcodes like `Ldc_I4` to load constants, `Stloc` to store values in local variables, and `Br` for branching.
   - Example: 
     ```csharp
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldc_I4, 5); // Load 5
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Stloc, x);  // Store in variable x
     ```

3. **Object Instantiation**:
   - Use `Newobj` to create instances of types and load them onto the evaluation stack.
   - Example: Construct a `StringBuilder` and call its methods using `Call` and `Callvirt`.

4. **Exception Handling**:
   - Use `BeginExceptionBlock`, `BeginCatchBlock`, and `BeginFinallyBlock` to handle exceptions in emitted IL.
   - Example: Catch a `NotSupportedException` and handle it appropriately.

5. **Emitting Assemblies and Types**:
   - Use `AssemblyBuilder` and `ModuleBuilder` to create dynamic assemblies and modules. Types can be defined using `TypeBuilder`.
   - Example: Define a class and its methods dynamically, then create an instance and invoke its methods.

6. **Saving Emitted Assemblies**:
   - Use the `Save` method on `AssemblyBuilder` to write the dynamically generated assembly to a file.
   - Ensure to specify the correct `AssemblyBuilderAccess` when creating the assembly.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice creating dynamic methods and using `ILGenerator` to emit IL instructions.
- Familiarize yourself with object instantiation and method invocation using reflection.
- Understand exception handling in IL and how to emit assemblies and types dynamically.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with dynamic code generation and reflection.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into dynamic methods and assembly generation in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on dynamic code generation, reflection, and IL in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Dynamic Assembly Generation and Reflection.Emit**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Dynamic Assembly Creation**:
   - Use `AssemblyBuilder` to create dynamic assemblies that can be saved to a specified directory. For example:
     ```csharp
     AssemblyBuilder assemBuilder = domain.DefineDynamicAssembly(aname, AssemblyBuilderAccess.RunAndSave, @"d:\assemblies");
     ```
   - Once saved, the dynamic assembly behaves like a regular assembly and can be referenced statically.

2. **TypeBuilder and MethodBuilder**:
   - Use `TypeBuilder` to define new types and `MethodBuilder` to define methods within those types. After defining methods, call `CreateType` to finalize the type.
   - Example of defining a method:
     ```csharp
     MethodBuilder mb = tb.DefineMethod("SquareRoot", MethodAttributes.Static | MethodAttributes.Public, typeof(double), new[] { typeof(double) });
     ```

3. **Emitting IL Instructions**:
   - Use `ILGenerator` to emit Intermediate Language (IL) instructions for method bodies. This includes loading arguments, calling methods, and returning values.
   - Example of emitting a method that calculates the square root:
     ```csharp
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldarg_0);
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Call, typeof(Math).GetMethod("Sqrt"));
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ret);
     ```

4. **Handling Parameters**:
   - Define parameters using `DefineParameter`, and for reference parameters, use `MakeByRefType`.
   - Example of a method with a reference parameter:
     ```csharp
     MethodBuilder mb = tb.DefineMethod("SquareRoot", MethodAttributes.Static | MethodAttributes.Public, null, new Type[] { typeof(double).MakeByRefType() });
     ```

5. **Creating Properties**:
   - Define properties using `DefineProperty` and attach getter and setter methods using `SetGetMethod` and `SetSetMethod`.
   - Example of defining a property:
     ```csharp
     PropertyBuilder prop = tb.DefineProperty("Text", PropertyAttributes.None, typeof(string), new Type[0]);
     ```

6. **Method Overriding and Hiding**:
   - Override base class methods by defining methods with the same name and signature, using `MethodAttributes.Virtual`.
   - Use `MethodAttributes.HideBySig` to ensure method hiding semantics are applied correctly.

7. **Finalizing Types**:
   - Call `CreateType` on a `TypeBuilder` to finalize the type and allow reflection on it. This seals the type, preventing further modifications.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice creating dynamic assemblies and types using `Reflection.Emit`.
- Familiarize yourself with emitting IL instructions and handling method parameters.
- Understand how to define properties and manage method overriding and hiding.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with dynamic assembly generation and reflection.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into `Reflection.Emit` and dynamic type creation in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on dynamic assembly generation, method emission, and reflection in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Dynamic Type Generation and Reflection.Emit**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Dynamic Field and Property Creation**:
   - Use `FieldBuilder` to define fields and `PropertyBuilder` to create properties dynamically. For example:
     ```csharp
     FieldBuilder field = tb.DefineField("_text", typeof(string), FieldAttributes.Private);
     PropertyBuilder prop = tb.DefineProperty("Text", PropertyAttributes.None, typeof(string), new Type[0]);
     ```

2. **Accessor Methods**:
   - Define getter and setter methods for properties using `MethodBuilder`. Use `ILGenerator` to emit IL instructions for these methods.
   - Example of a getter:
     ```csharp
     MethodBuilder getter = tb.DefineMethod("get_Text", MethodAttributes.Public | MethodAttributes.SpecialName, typeof(string), new Type[0]);
     ILGenerator getGen = getter.GetILGenerator();
     getGen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldarg_0);
     getGen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldfld, field);
     getGen.Emit(OpCodes.Ret);
     ```

3. **Testing Properties**:
   - After defining properties and methods, create an instance of the type and test the property:
     ```csharp
     Type t = tb.CreateType();
     object o = Activator.CreateInstance(t);
     t.GetProperty("Text").SetValue(o, "Good emissions!", null);
     string text = (string)t.GetProperty("Text").GetValue(o, null);
     Console.WriteLine(text); // Outputs: Good emissions!
     ```

4. **Constructor Definition**:
   - Define constructors using `DefineConstructor`. If you need to initialize fields, do so within the constructor:
     ```csharp
     ConstructorBuilder c = tb.DefineConstructor(MethodAttributes.Public, CallingConventions.Standard, new Type[0]);
     ILGenerator gen = c.GetILGenerator();
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldarg_0);
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ldc_I4, 4000);
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Stfld, field);
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Ret);
     ```

5. **Base Constructor Calls**:
   - Explicitly call base class constructors if subclassing, as they are not automatically invoked:
     ```csharp
     ConstructorInfo baseConstr = typeof(A).GetConstructor(new Type[0]);
     gen.Emit(OpCodes.Call, baseConstr);
     ```

6. **Attaching Attributes**:
   - Use `CustomAttributeBuilder` to attach custom attributes to fields or properties:
     ```csharp
     var att = new CustomAttributeBuilder(attConstructor, new object[] { "FirstName" }, new PropertyInfo[] { attType.GetProperty("Namespace"), attType.GetProperty("Order") }, new object[] { "http://test/", 3 });
     myFieldBuilder.SetCustomAttribute(att);
     ```

7. **Defining Generic Methods**:
   - Use `DefineGenericParameters` on `MethodBuilder` to create generic methods:
     ```csharp
     GenericTypeParameterBuilder[] genericParams = mb.DefineGenericParameters("T");
     ```

8. **Defining Generic Types**:
   - Similar to methods, use `DefineGenericParameters` on `TypeBuilder` to create generic types:
     ```csharp
     TypeBuilder tb = modBuilder.DefineType("Widget", TypeAttributes.Public);
     GenericTypeParameterBuilder[] genericParams = tb.DefineGenericParameters("T");
     tb.DefineField("Value", genericParams[0], FieldAttributes.Public);
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice defining fields, properties, and constructors dynamically using `Reflection.Emit`.
- Familiarize yourself with emitting IL instructions for property accessors and constructors.
- Understand how to attach attributes and define generic methods and types.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with dynamic type generation and reflection.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into `Reflection.Emit` and dynamic type creation in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on dynamic type generation, property and method emission, and reflection in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Advanced Reflection and Dynamic Type Generation**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Uncreated Closed Generics**:
   - When emitting methods that use closed generic types, you cannot directly call `GetConstructor` on a generic type closed with an uncreated type builder. Instead, use `TypeBuilder.GetConstructor` to obtain the constructor for the closed generic type.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     Type variableType = typeof(List<>).MakeGenericType(tb);
     ConstructorInfo unbound = typeof(List<>).GetConstructor(new Type[0]);
     ConstructorInfo ci = TypeBuilder.GetConstructor(variableType, unbound);
     ```

2. **Circular Dependencies**:
   - To create types that reference each other, define both types without calling `CreateType` until both are fully populated. This allows for mutual references without causing exceptions.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     TypeBuilder aBuilder = modBuilder.DefineType("A");
     TypeBuilder bBuilder = modBuilder.DefineType("B");
     aBuilder.DefineField("Bee", bBuilder, FieldAttributes.Public);
     bBuilder.DefineField("Aye", aBuilder, FieldAttributes.Public);
     Type realA = aBuilder.CreateType();
     Type realB = bBuilder.CreateType();
     ```

3. **Handling Structs with Circular References**:
   - Circular references between structs are not allowed. However, using generic structs can help manage such relationships. Define a struct that can be empty to avoid circular dependency issues.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     class A { S<B> Bee; }
     class B { S<A> Aye; }
     ```

4. **TypeResolve Event**:
   - Use the `TypeResolve` event to handle circular dependencies when creating types. This allows the type builder to resolve types that are not yet created.
   - Example:
     ```csharp
     AppDomain.CurrentDomain.TypeResolve += handler;
     ```

5. **Parsing IL**:
   - Use `GetMethodBody` to obtain information about a method's IL, including local variables and exception handling. This can be useful for profiling and analyzing code.
   - Example of disassembling IL:
     ```csharp
     public static string Disassemble(MethodBase method) => new Disassembler(method).Dis();
     ```

6. **Disassembler Implementation**:
   - Create a disassembler class that processes IL bytecode and outputs the corresponding instructions. This involves reading the byte array and interpreting the IL instructions.
   - Example structure:
     ```csharp
     public class Disassembler {
         // Implementation details...
     }
     ```

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice creating and managing closed generics and handling circular dependencies in dynamic type generation.
- Familiarize yourself with the `TypeResolve` event for resolving types during dynamic assembly creation.
- Explore parsing IL and implementing a disassembler to analyze method bodies.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with advanced reflection and dynamic type generation.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into reflection, IL generation, and dynamic assembly management in C#. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on advanced reflection techniques, dynamic type generation, and IL parsing in C#.

**C# 7.0 Coding Interview Preparation Summary: Reflection, IL Parsing, and Dynamic Programming**

**Core Concepts:**

1. **Disassembler Implementation**:
   - Create a disassembler to read and interpret IL (Intermediate Language) instructions. Use a static dictionary to map opcodes for efficient access.
   - Example of initializing opcodes:
     ```csharp
     static Dictionary<short, OpCode> _opcodes = new Dictionary<short, OpCode>();
     static Disassembler() {
         foreach (FieldInfo fi in typeof(OpCodes).GetFields(BindingFlags.Public | BindingFlags.Static)) {
             if (typeof(OpCode).IsAssignableFrom(fi.FieldType)) {
                 OpCode code = (OpCode)fi.GetValue(null);
                 if (code.OpCodeType != OpCodeType.Nternal) _opcodes.Add(code.Value, code);
             }
         }
     }
     ```

2. **Reading IL Instructions**:
   - Each IL instruction consists of an opcode followed by an operand. Implement methods to read these components and format the output.
   - Example of reading an opcode:
     ```csharp
     OpCode ReadOpCode() {
         byte byteCode = _il[_pos++];
         if (_opcodes.ContainsKey(byteCode)) return _opcodes[byteCode];
         // Handle 2-byte opcodes...
     }
     ```

3. **Operand Handling**:
   - Determine the length of operands based on their type and format them accordingly. Special cases include member references, strings, and branch targets.
   - Example of formatting operands:
     ```csharp
     string FormatOperand(OpCode c, int operandLength) {
         if (operandLength == 4) return Get4ByteOperand(c);
         // Handle other operand types...
     }
     ```

4. **Dynamic Language Runtime (DLR)**:
   - The DLR facilitates dynamic binding in C#, allowing for interoperability between statically and dynamically typed languages. It provides runtime services for dynamic programming.
   - Call sites are created for dynamic expressions, enabling efficient member resolution at runtime.

5. **Dynamic Member Overload Resolution**:
   - The DLR optimizes dynamic member resolution through call-site caching, reducing overhead during dynamic binding.

6. **Custom Binding and Dynamic Objects**:
   - Implement custom dynamic objects by overriding methods from `DynamicObject` or using `ExpandoObject` for flexible member definitions.

**Preparation Tips**:
- Practice implementing a disassembler to parse IL instructions and format operands.
- Familiarize yourself with the DLR and its role in dynamic programming and interoperability.
- Explore creating dynamic objects and handling dynamic member resolution.

**Resources**:
- Use Visual Studio and LINQPad for hands-on coding practice with reflection, IL parsing, and dynamic programming.
- Refer to Microsoft’s .NET documentation for deeper insights into reflection, dynamic programming, and the DLR. 

This summary serves as a guide for effectively preparing for coding interviews with a focus on reflection, IL parsing, and dynamic programming in C#.
